## Christoph Gottlieb von Murr

# Literary Reports

->on<-

## the History

->of<-

# So-called Gold-making.

---

> "In our age, those who loudly proclaim the gold-making art <term>chrysopoeia: the alchemical process of transmuting base metals into gold</term>, and try to convince the unlearned that they possess it, are all sellers of smoke <note>original: *fumiuenduli* — a historical term for swindlers or those who sell empty promises</note> and seekers of profit, whose minds are possessed by the desire for vain boasting and money." <note>Translated from the original Latin: "Nostro Saeculo, qui artem auriferam crepant, imperitisque, eam se tenere, persuadere conantur, omnes sunt fumiuenduli, lucripetae, quorum animos vanae gloriationis et nummorum cupido obsedit."</note> *Werner Rolfinck's Chemistry, page 436.* <note>Werner Rolfinck (1599–1673) was a German physician and chemist who was instrumental in transitioning chemistry from alchemical mysticism to a formal academic discipline.</note>

---

->Leipzig,<-
->by Paul Gotthelf Kummer.<-
->1805.<-

<summary>This title page introduces Christoph Gottlieb von Murr's historical and bibliographic study of alchemy, published in 1805. The inclusion of a quote by Werner Rolfinck reflects the Enlightenment-era skepticism toward the practical possibility of transmuting metals into gold.</summary>

VI.

---

# Preface

---

After the fantasies of many centuries, the truth about alchemy—namely, that it is nothing but deception and fraud—has finally been brought into the light of day.

Chemistry was still in its infancy until the time of <term>Constantine the Great<note>Constantine I (reigned 306–337 AD), the Roman Emperor who legalized Christianity. The author suggests scientific progress was minimal until late antiquity.</note></term> *). For what one reads regarding <term>Hermes Trismegistus<note>A legendary figure, supposedly an ancient Egyptian priest and philosopher, to whom many foundational (and often mystical) alchemical texts were attributed.</note></term> is nothing but fairy tales.

---
*) Torbern Bergmann’s <term>Dissertation on the Origins of Chemistry<note>original Latin title: "diss. de primordiis Chemiae"</note></term>. <term>Uppsala<note>original Latin: "Upsaliae"</note></term>, 1779. <term>Quarto format<note>original: "4.", referring to the book size where each sheet is folded twice to create four leaves.</note></term>. German translation by Mr. Joh.

<summary>This preface introduces the author's skeptical view of alchemy, characterizing it as a long-standing fraud while dating the true origins of chemistry to late antiquity. It cites the Enlightenment-era scholar Torbern Bergmann to debunk the mythical origins of the craft.</summary>
<keywords>preface, alchemy, history of chemistry, Constantine the Great, Hermes Trismegistus, Torbern Bergmann, Enlightenment science</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, shifting from the ancient mythical origins of alchemy to the genuine scientific progress made from the Renaissance through the Enlightenment.</meta>

IV.

# Preface

<meta>catchword: Thereby</meta>

The actual growth of chemical knowledge proceeds from <term>Paracelsus<note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note></term> *) up to <term>Becher<note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682), a German chemist and physician known for his development of the theory that would become the phlogiston theory.</note></term> and <term>Stahl<note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), the chemist who formalized the phlogiston theory, which suggested a fire-like element was contained within combustible bodies.</note></term>, and from them once again to the great discoverers of <term>artificial types of air<note>original: "erkünstelter Luftarten." In the 18th century, "airs" was the term used for what we now call gases (e.g., oxygen was "dephlogisticated air").</note></term> and the expanders of <term>salt chemistry<note>The branch of chemistry concerned with the composition and reactions of acids, bases, and salts.</note></term>: Macbride, Priestley, Ulrici, Marggraf, Macquer, Lavoisier, and many others.

Chemistry was first taught publicly only at the end of the 16th century. The first textbook was written by <term>Libavius<note>Andreas Libavius (c. 1555–1616), whose work "Alchymia" is considered the first systematic textbook of chemistry.</note></term>, Frankfurt 1597, in <term>quarto format<note>original: "4.", a book size where sheets are folded twice to form four leaves.</note></term>, and expanded in 1606 in <term>folio format<note>A large book size where sheets are folded only once.</note></term>. In 1780, I possessed all of his chemical writings in my library. Being ignorant in the various branches of medicine, many chemists administered doses without proper judgment, and thereby frequently caused tragic consequences for the sick.

---

Translated by Christian Wiegleb, with additions. Berlin and Stettin, 1792. Large <term>octavo format<note>original: "gr. 8.", a book size roughly 8 to 10 inches tall.</note></term>.

*) I shall say more about this in my treatise on his life and writings.

<summary>This page outlines the chronological evolution of chemistry from Paracelsus to the late 18th-century "pneumatic" chemists like Priestley and Lavoisier. It also notes the academic professionalization of the field and warns of the historical dangers of chemists practicing medicine without proper training.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, Becher, Stahl, Libavius, history of chemistry, gas chemistry, medical ethics, Christian Wiegleb, scientific textbooks</keywords>

v

# Preface

...they made chemical preparations so notorious that in the year 1566 in Paris, by a decree of Parliament, no preparation of <term>antimony<note>original: "Antimonium oder Spießglanze." Antimony was a controversial remedy in the 16th century, often used as a powerful emetic (to induce vomiting).</note></term> was permitted to be used for the sick. Although many physicians soon after came to the defense of chemistry, it was not until 1673 that Johann Matte was appointed the first demonstrator of chemistry in Montpellier *).

When <term>Stahl<note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734), a central figure in the transition from alchemy to chemistry.</note></term> transformed metals into <term>calx<note>original: "Kalk." In historical chemistry, "calx" refers to the powdery substance left after a metal has been roasted; we now know this as a metal oxide.</note></term>, and turned this back into metals again, chemists could finally see how much their art depended on not inventing things about nature, but rather seeking through experience that which reason places within nature. Such men, who have worked with their own hands, are to be preferred over all those pharmaceutical chemists who merely theorize.

If only all lovers of alchemy would proceed as did the one who, in the year 1785 in <insert>Silesia</insert> <note>original: "Schle="; likely "Schlesien" (Silesia), truncated at the page break.</note>

---

*) <note>original: "Astruc Mémoires pour servir à l'hist. de la Faculté de Médécine de Montpellier; p. 69."</note> Jean Astruc, *Memoirs to serve the history of the Faculty of Medicine of Montpellier*; p. 69.

<summary>This page discusses the historical transition of chemistry from a banned medical practice in 16th-century Paris to an empirical science championed by Georg Ernst Stahl, emphasizing practical experimentation over mere theory.</summary>
<keywords>antimony ban, Paris Parliament 1566, Johann Matte, Montpellier, Georg Ernst Stahl, calx, calcination, empirical chemistry, Jean Astruc</keywords>

VI

# Preface

[Sile]sia, the late Mr. von Siegroth! As an excellent <term>estate manager<note>original: "Oekonom." In this historical context, an "Oekonom" was someone skilled in the management of land, finances, and household resources.</note></term>, he was able to indulge his inclination for the <term>hermetic art<note>original: "hermetischen Kunst." This is a traditional name for alchemy, named after the mythical figure Hermes Trismegistus, who was thought to be the father of the craft.</note></term> all the more securely, and yet still leave to each of his twelve heirs at least 15,000 <term>Reichsthalers<note>original: "Rthlr." The Reichsthaler was a standard silver coin used across the German states of the Holy Roman Empire; 15,000 was a very substantial sum.</note></term>.

->Nuremberg, July 14th<-
->1804.<-

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page appears to be a continuation of a historical survey regarding the origins of alchemy and the transmutation of metals.</meta>

<sig>A 2</sig>

In the earliest times, it was believed in the Orient that the transformation of base metals into nobler ones could be achieved. The Chinese possess a great alchemical work: *The Guarded Dragon’s Gate of Pî schu* <note>original: *Porta Draconis custodita του Pî schu*</note>. This name designates what we express through *Basilius Valentinus* <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, likely a pseudonym, whose writings were highly influential in the development of chemistry.</note>, the collector of gold-cooking recipes <note>original: *Goldkocherey-Recepten*</note>. They imagine <note>or "fable"</note> that a dragon guards the place of true science, or of the <term>philosopher’s stone|The legendary substance supposed to be capable of turning base metals into gold</term>. This absurd collection is in the Paris National Library and consists of 163 booklets in twenty volumes, or covers. *Fourmont, Chinese Grammar <note>original: *Gramm. Sin.*</note>, page 479.*

From India, the obsession with refining <note>original: *Veredlungssucht*</note> metals traveled to Egypt, and from there to the Greeks and Arabs.

Among the Greeks, *Pelagius*, [writing] on the divine and holy art, is probably the oldest. The whitening effect of arsenic was recorded by *Stephan of Alexandria* (around the year 615 AD); so that, strictly speaking, people have prattled about gold-making since the seventh century.

The learned physician *Johann Stephan Bernard* at Leiden published in 1745, as an appendix to *Palladius’s Concise Synopsis on Fevers* <note>original: *Palladii concisa Synopsi de Febribus*</note>, the [writings] of *Jakob*...

<summary>This page explores the historical roots of alchemy, tracing its movement from China and India to Egypt and Greece, while citing specific manuscripts and early practitioners like Stephan of Alexandria.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, China, gold-making, Basilius Valentinus, philosopher's stone, Pelagius, Stephan of Alexandria, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of historical alchemical manuscripts, following the mention of the physician Johann Stephan Bernard and his work on the synopsis of Palladius.</meta>

Philippe d’Orville, regarding the copies of **Chemical Glosses** <note>original: *Glossis chemicis*</note> and passages from **alchemical poems** communicated to him, which were at that time in the St. Mark’s Library in Venice and are now in Paris. The parchment codex dates to the twelfth century and contains 169 folio leaves.

The excellent Abbate Morelli provides a precise description of it in the first part of his *Library of Greek and Latin Manuscripts of the Royal St. Mark’s Library of Venice* <note>original: *Bibliotheca manuscripta graeca et latina Bibliothecae regiae diui Marci Venetiarum* (Bassani, 1802, large octavo)</note>, pages 172–178. Among other things, it contains nine <term>Actions|The Greek word *praxeis* (πράξεις) used here refers to lectures or lessons rather than chemical processes</term> by **Stephan of Alexandria**, the "Universal Philosopher," which Domenico Pizzimenti published in Latin in Padua in 1573. From a manuscript in the Breslau library, Hofrath Gruner had the first lecture printed in Greek in 1777.

**Heliodorus’s** iambic verses to the Emperor Theodosius, concerning the mystical art of the philosophers, were brought to light by Fabricius in volume VI of the *Greek Library* <note>original: *Biblioth. gr.*</note>, page 790; excerpts with variations were provided by Bernard on page 151. On page 154, one reads excerpts from **Theophrastus’s** iambic verses *On the Divine Art* <note>original: *de Arte divina*</note>, as well as from several others.

The philosopher <term>Anepigraphus|A Greek term meaning 'unlabeled' or 'untitled,' here used to refer to an anonymous author</term>’s treatise *On the Whitening of the Divine Water* <note>original: *περι θειου υδατος της λευκοσεως*</note> is also found in a Vienna codex <note>according to Lambecius, Book VI, page 397</note> and in a Florentine one <note>according to Bandini, Volume III, page 352</note>. In these same volumes, there are also *Chemical Experiments* <note>original: *Experimenta chemica*</note> by an unnamed author, as well as a treatise *On the Divine Water, to Sergius* by an "Anonymous Christian," and on the consistency of gold...

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic catalog of ancient Greek alchemical manuscripts and begins a discussion on the history of gold refining techniques.</meta>

...[regarding] **On the Stability of Gold** <note>original: *περι ευσταθείας του χρυσου*</note>, found in Bandini, Volume III, page 350. Bernard provided the beginning on page 163.

The **Alphabetical Lexicon of Gold-Making** <note>original: *Lexicon alphabeticum Chrysopoeiae*</note> is provided by Bernard, pages 120–148. These are glosses <note>marginal or interlinear explanations of difficult words</note> which Du Cange also cites.

Excerpts from the Alexandrian philosopher **Olympiodorus’s** work on *Zosimus’s Chapters to Theodore* can be read in Fabricius, Volume XII, page 764; on page 765 is an **Anonymous Chemical Treatise** <note>original: *Anonymi Syngramma Chemicum*</note>. Page 766 contains **Pappus** *On Chemical Matters* <note>original: *de re Chemica*</note> and **Hierotheus** *On the Sacred Art* <note>original: *de sacra Arte*</note>.

**Zosimus** on *Apparatus and Furnaces* <note>original: *περι οργανων και καμινων*</note> and his fragment *On the Divine Water* <note>original: *περι του θείου ύδατος*</note> were noted by Lambecius, Book VI, page 405.

Mr. **Ameilhon** <margin>\*) *Memoirs of the Academy of Inscriptions and Belles-Lettres*, Vol. 46, page 501. Paris 1793. 4. *Göttingen Gazette*, 1793, page 1808.</margin> demonstrated that while the ancients already knew of the use of lead for the melting and refining of gold, they did not apply it in a fully efficient manner. **Geber** <note>The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an influential 8th-century chemist whose works bridge ancient and medieval science</note> taught this application more effectively. Even in his time, <term>cupels|Shallow, porous vessels used in assaying to separate precious metals from impurities like lead</term> <note>original: *Kapellen*</note> were prepared just as they are today. However, the ancients were not familiar with the "moist separation" <margin>\*\*) It was not until the year 1400 that the Venetians used <term>parting water|Nitric acid, used to dissolve silver and leave gold behind</term> <note>original: *Scheidewasser*</note> for this purpose.</margin> of silver from gold, though they did know <term>cementation|A dry refining process where gold is heated with salts to remove base metals</term>. Yet, during this process, they allowed the silver to be lost because they did not know how to extract it from the slag <note>The stony waste matter left over after smelting</note>. The **white gold** <note>original: *λευκος χρυσος*</note>, or the "white gold" of **Herodotus**—

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of metallurgy and chemical substances, transitioning from ancient Greek knowledge to the contributions of Arab scholars and the subsequent arrival of "Arabist" Latin translations in the Middle Ages.</meta>

...is **Pliny’s Electrum**, or a mixture of gold and silver, which the **Abbot Gedoyn** confused with **amber** <note>The Greek and Latin word *elektron/electrum* refers both to the naturally occurring alloy of gold and silver and to fossilized resin (amber)</note> in his translation of **Pausanias**.

Already 2,300 years ago, cinnabar from the mercury mines at **Almaden** in Spain was brought to Rome in the form of a sand \*).

**Abu Yusuf Al-Kindi** <note>Al-Kindi (c. 801–873 AD), known as "the Philosopher of the Arabs," was one of the first to argue against the possibility of the alchemical transformation of base metals into gold</note> wrote a book in 890 AD **refuting those who boast of the art of making gold and silver** <note>original Latin: *quo refelluntur, qui auri et argenti conficiendi artem jactant*</note>. Casiri, *Arabic-Spanish Library of the Escorial*, Vol. I, p. 356.

**Dhul-Nun al-Misri** <note>original: *Zulnun Ibn Ibrahim*</note>, from **Akhmim** in **Egypt**, who was in no way inferior to **Geber** in chemical knowledge, wrote his own work on chemical experiments. Casiri, in the place cited, p. 441.

**"Red Sulfur"** <note>original Arabic: *Kebrat al ahhmad*; Latin: *Sulphur rubrum*</note>, say the Arabs, instead of alchemical gold; for example, **Abu Bakr ibn Tufail** <note>original: *Abi Dschafar Ibn Tofail*</note> in his famous philosophical epistle of **Hayy ibn Yaqdhan**, p. 14 of the second improved edition by **Pocock, Oxford, 1700.** 4to.

Now the **Arabists** <note>Scholars in the 12th and 13th centuries who translated Arabic scientific and philosophical works into Latin</note> pressed in. That wretched **Assembly of the Philosophers** <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*, one of the earliest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, framed as a debate between pre-Socratic philosophers</note> was already compiled in the 12th century. It is cited by **Alanus**, **Albertus Magnus**, and others of the 13th century. **Bernard of Treviso** says that **Parmenides** set him on the right path within this **Assembly**. Mere vanity!

---

\*) **On the Mercury Mining at Almaden**, by **Johann Martin Hoppensack**, Royal Spanish Director of Mines. With copperplate engravings. **Weimar 1790.** 8vo.

<summary>This page explores the etymology of "Electrum" and catalogs influential Arab chemists like Al-Kindi and Dhul-Nun al-Misri, while dismissing early Latin alchemical compilations like the Turba Philosophorum as "mere vanity."</summary>
<keywords>Electrum, Almaden, Cinnabar, Al-Kindi, Dhul-Nun al-Misri, Red Sulfur, Turba Philosophorum, Bernard of Treviso, Arabists</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of medieval alchemical thought, moving from the Arabists to the views of high medieval scholasticism and the early chemical experiments of the 13th century.</meta>

The following passage from **Albertus** (born 1205, died 1280) <note>Albertus Magnus, a Dominican friar and bishop who was one of the most influential thinkers of the Middle Ages</note> may find a place here instead of a hundred others, and bear witness to his poor opinion of alchemy.

*Albertus Magnus, On Metals, Book III, chapter 9, page 146, edited by Walther Hermann Ryff, Strasbourg, 1541. Octavo.* <note>original Latin: *Albert M. de rebus metallicis... Argentorati*</note>

Alchemy, however, proceeds in this manner: it destroys one thing by removing its specific form and, with the help of what is already in the material, introduces the form of another. For this reason, the best of all alchemical operations is the one that proceeds from the same sources as nature does—such as the purification of sulfur through boiling and sublimation, the cleaning of quicksilver <term>quicksilver: an archaic name for mercury</term>, and the thorough mixing of these with the metallic matter. For in these, through their inherent powers, the form of every metal is induced. However, those who use white substances to whiten and yellow substances to gild, while the original nature of the metal remains in the material, are beyond doubt deceivers. They do not create true gold or true silver. Almost all alchemists proceed in this way, either entirely or in part. For this reason, I had it tested: the alchemical gold that came to me, and likewise the silver, after it had endured six or seven fires, was immediately consumed and wasted away upon further heating, returning almost to dross.

**Ramon Lull** (born on the island of Mallorca in 1235, died 1315) wrote a work called *Experiments* <note>original: *Experimenta*</note>. **Boerhaave** <note>Herman Boerhaave (1668–1738), a famous Dutch physician and chemist</note> admired his insight into the explanation of nature in all three kingdoms <note>The "three kingdoms" refers to the traditional classification of nature into Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral</note>. "Allow me to cite Raymond Lull," (he says in Volume I of his *Elements of Chemistry* <note>original: *Elem. chem.*</note>), "in that treatise which he calls *Experiments*..."

<meta>catchword: calls</meta>

<summary>This page quotes Albertus Magnus's skeptical view on alchemy, specifically his observation that "alchemical" gold fails to survive repeated heating, and introduces Ramon Lull's chemical experiments.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, Ramon Lull, Boerhaave, alchemy, metallurgy, gold testing, mercury, sulfur, scholasticism, history of chemistry</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Ramon Lull (Raymundus Lullius), specifically focusing on his scientific method as praised by Boerhaave and his relationship to his teacher, Arnald de Villanova. It concludes with an examination of the "rose noble" legend and the author's own experience with Lullian manuscripts.</meta>

...life. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a Latin quotation from Herman Boerhaave regarding Ramon Lull.</note> You may see with what clarity he explains there, through naked experiments and without any circumlocution, deceit, or fictions, the natures and actions of animals, fossils, and things growing from the earth. Then indeed, speak candidly: where have you found physics treated in such a way? "Through those demonstrations," he says, "which present bodies resolved by our art <note>The "art" here refers to alchemy or the early chemical processes of separating substances.</note> to the eyes and minds, we express our assent infinitely more effectively than by any force of arguments; through them, we do what we say, and we perform what we teach."

He recognized *Arnald de Villanova* <note>Arnald de Villanova (c. 1240–1311) was a Catalan physician and alchemist, one of the most famous medical authorities of the Middle Ages.</note> as his master. "I received these things," he says *), "and I held them from the most Serene King Robert of Naples, under the seal of secrecy; these experiments he himself had obtained from the most skilled *Arnald de Villanova*, who deserves to be called the fountain of science, because he flourished in all sciences beyond all other men."

Regarding the ridiculous fable of the rose nobles <term>rose noble: a 15th-century English gold coin. A popular legend claimed Ramon Lull created the gold for these coins through alchemy for King Edward III.</term> of *Ramon*, one need only read what *Wegener* says in his *The Inept Adept* <note>original Latin: *Adepto inepto*</note>, page 339, and what the late *Wiegleb* says in his *Investigation of Alchemy*, page 216 and following. Even *Paracelsus* judged: "It is thought that *Lull* falsely fabricated this gold from which the rose nobles were made." <note>original Latin: *Lullium hoc aurum, ex quo Rosenobel facti, falso fabricasse putari.*</note>

Ich hatte 1780 zwey seltne alchemistische Papier-Codices <term>codex: a manuscript in book form, rather than a scroll.</term>, welche jetzt in einer berühmten Fürstlichen Bibliothek sind. Der eine ist in Frankreich, der andere in Italien geschrieben. Beyde enthielten die *works of Ramon Lull*.

---

*) In *His Testament*, and in the *Preface to the Operative Art* <note>original Latin: *Testamento suo* and *Praefat. artis operatiuae*</note>. *Brucker*, *Critical History of Philosophy*, Volume IV, Part I, page 15.

<summary>This page evaluates the alchemical reputation of Ramon Lull, contrasting his empirical scientific clarity with the legendary fables of gold-making, such as the rose noble coins. It highlights his connection to Arnald de Villanova and the existence of rare Lullian manuscripts in 18th-century libraries.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Lull, Arnald de Villanova, Boerhaave, rose noble, alchemy, Paracelsus, manuscript codices, King Robert of Naples, Wiegleb</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the cataloging of alchemical manuscripts attributed to Ramon Lull. The previous page discussed the legends surrounding Lull and his teacher, Arnald de Villanova; here, the author begins a detailed inventory of a specific 14th-century manuscript and its contents, comparing them to known printed editions.</meta>

The first manuscript from the 14th century contained 168 small folio leaves. It is contemporary with Lullius and is written mostly in the original Spanish language.

*Fol. 1. Various alchemical recipes; for example: Operation for the white work <note>original: "Operatio ad album." In alchemy, the "white work" (albedo) is the stage of purification leading to the creation of silver, prior to the final "red" stage (rubedo) for gold.</note>. The Work of Saint Thomas Aquinas. Purification of raw mercury. A successful operation and increase.*

*Fol. 3. b. Composition of the "water of life" <term>aqua vitae: "water of life," a term used by alchemists to refer to concentrated alcohol or potent distillates believed to have medicinal or transformative properties.</term> by Master Raymond, or the Great Art of the Transmutation of Metals.*

*Fol. 4. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Arnaldus de Villa Nova (Arnald of Villanova), transitioning from the bibliography of Raymond Lully to Arnald’s reputation as an alchemist and his legitimate medical contributions. Adepts <note>highly skilled alchemists who claimed to have discovered the secret of the philosopher's stone</note> wish to provide proof—based on the roaring lion and the dragon biting its own tail seen there in raised relief work—that he accomplished the Great Work <note>the "Magnum Opus," or the process of creating the philosopher's stone</note> and made gold in that very place!! Pope Clement V says in his bull issued in Avignon on September 8, 1305 *), *Concerning the Election of the Chancellor of the Faculty of Medicine in Montpellier: Having discussed these matters with our beloved sons, Arnald of Villanova and John of Alesto, **) our physician and chaplain, who formerly governed [the faculty] for a long time, etc.* <note>original Latin: "de Electione Cancellarii Facultatis Medicinae in Montepessulano: Habita super his, cum dilectis filiis, Arnoldo de Villanoua, et Ioanne de Alesto, Physico et Capellano nostro, qui olim diu rexerunt etc."</note> Arnald was 73 years old at that time.

Worth more than all the daydreams of his *Testament, Operative Art,* and *The Philosophers' Rose Garden* <note>original Latin titles: "Testamenti, Artis operatiuae and Rosarii philosophorum"</note> is his discovery of spirits of wine, *Water of Life [Aqua Vitae] or Burning Water [Aqua Ardens]***).* <note>Arnald is historically credited with being one of the first to describe the distillation of alcohol for medicinal purposes.</note>

The gold-makers <note>alchemists</note> boast loudly of the testimony of the jurist Johannes Andreae regarding the small gold bars Arnald produced in Rome, of which Thomas Murch writes in the preface to *The Works of Arnald* (Lyon, 1509 folio): *I omit the fact that he was so skilled in the secrets of nature that he possessed the true Alchemy and produced golden plates, which were not inferior to the finest gold, as Johannes Andreae testifies under the heading of the crime of forgery.* <note>original Latin: "Omitto, quod arcana naturae adeo calluit, ut veram Alchimiam teneret faceretque laminas aureas, non cedentes perfectissimo auro, ut Iohannes Andreas in titulo de falsi crimine attestatur." The reference to the "crime of forgery" concerns a legal debate over whether alchemical gold could legally be used as money.</note> The passage, which is preceded by all sorts of clever remarks for and against alchemy, appears in Andreae's *Additions*—

<meta>This page continues the legal and philosophical defense of alchemy and mining rights, transitioning from the previous page's discussion on the legality of smelting gold to arguing that alchemical transmutation is a natural process rather than a fraudulent one.</meta>

This increases the public wealth. <note>original: "hoc auget rem publicam." A standard legal justification for state-supported industries.</note> Code [of Justinian], regarding miners, Law 1, Book 11. And these <term>miners</term> <note>original: "metallarii," referring to those who work in mines or with metals</note> are privileged against the aforementioned single law regarding <term>treasure</term> <note>original: "thesaurus." In Roman law, "treasure" referred to hidden property whose owner was unknown.</note> and the Digest, on acquiring ownership, Law 3, such that they may enter another’s land even against the owner's will, as seen in the Code, regarding miners, Law 3. 

Nor do they change one species into another different one, but they make one species of metal better, such as silver or gold from tin or copper. Nor is it a wonder, since we see silk produced from worms and glass from plants <note>original: "ex herba vitrum." This refers to the use of plant ashes, such as from glasswort or seaweed, in the glassmaking process.</note>, as the *Book on the Properties of Things* <note>original: "liber de proprietatibus rerum," the famous 13th-century encyclopedia by Bartholomaeus Anglicus (Bartholomew the Englishman).</note> states in the section on the <term>alchemist</term>, where it says that all metals proceed from <term>sulfur</term> and <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "sulphure et argento viuo." In medieval alchemy, the "Sulfur-Mercury theory" held that these two principles combined in the earth to form all metals.</note>. 

But by the power of the elements, there is a greater influence in one place than in another, from which it happens that in one place the ore is tin, in another silver, and in another gold. Since, therefore, **art imitates nature** (Digest, on adoption), alchemists do not seem to sin if, through the power of herbs, stones, or other elements, they improve a metal into a more precious metal, since both were of the same species and origin. This is supported by the *Institutes*, [section on] how obligations are dissolved, at the beginning: it is fitting that what was brought in by words be dissolved by words. <note>A legal maxim suggesting that processes should be undone or transformed using the same principles by which they were created.</note>

And **Augustine** says in his book *The City of God*, that within corporeal things, through all the elements, there are certain hidden **seed-like principles** <note>original: "seminariae rationes." An Augustinian concept suggesting that God placed the potential for all future forms within the initial creation, which then "unfold" over time.</note> which, when given a temporal or causal opportunity, burst forth into species according to their own modes and limits. This is found literally in [the Decretum], Question 26, canon 5, "nor a wonder." 

Likewise, we see that the penalty is not great for one who even knowingly gives copper for gold. For he is held for the crime of **swindling** <note>original: "stellionatus." In Roman law, a catch-all term for fraud or "shifty" behavior that didn't fit into specific categories like theft.</note>, the penalty for which is extraordinary <note>original: "extraordinaria," meaning the punishment was not fixed by law but left to the discretion of the judge.</note>. Digest, on the action of pledges, Law 1, at the end, and on...

<summary>This page presents a legal and philosophical defense of alchemy, arguing that because art imitates nature and metals share a common origin in sulfur and mercury, transmutation is a legitimate improvement of material rather than forgery. It cites Roman law (Justinian's Code and Digest), medieval encyclopedias, and St. Augustine to justify both mining rights and alchemical practice.</summary>
<keywords>mining law, alchemy, sulfur-mercury theory, Bartholomaeus Anglicus, St. Augustine, seminariae rationes, stellionatus, forgery, legal precedent</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legal and historical standing of alchemy, moving from general principles to specific historical figures like Arnaldus de Villa Nova and 14th-century alchemical recipes.</meta>

On the crime of <term>fraud</term> <note>original: "crim. stell." (crimine stellionatus). In Roman law, this referred to "swindling" or various forms of trickery.</note>, Law 2, and more. In our days, we had Master Arnaldus de Villa Nova at the Roman Court, a supreme physician and theologian, regarding whom I wrote a legal opinion <note>original: "consilium"</note> on the <term>observance of fasts</term> <note>original: "de ob. ie." (de observatione ieiuniorum)</note>; he was also a very great alchemist who allowed the little rods of gold that he made to be submitted to every possible test.

Symphorien Champier <note>also known as Symphorian Campegius (1471–1538), a French physician and humanist</note>, in his *Life of Arnaldus de Villa Nova*—which is printed as a preface to Arnaldus’s works (Venice 1505; Lyon 1509, 1520, and 1532; and Basel 1581 and 1585, folio)—does not think much of Arnaldus’s gold-making. "But what is said," he writes, "concerning the alchemy of Arnaldus, is false and full of fables, and unworthy of such a great man." And what, then, were those little gold rods or gold foils? They were either heavily gilded iron or copper, or false gold that would never have stood the test of the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Kapelle." A cupel is a small, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals through intense heat.</note>.

And how did the gold-making recipes of the 14th century look in general? Here is one. Judge the whole by this small sample! <note>original: "Ex ungue leonem!"—literally, "The lion [is known] by its claw."</note>

Likely referring to borax or a similar flux used in metalworking.</note> and one ounce of <term>sublimated silver</term><- <note>original: "Argentum sublimatum." In alchemical texts, this often referred to mercury that had been "fixed" or a specific chemical preparation intended to resemble silver.</note>

---

*) That is, *on the observance of fasts*; Book III of the Decretals, Title 46. In the commentary of Johannes Andreae <note>a renowned 14th-century expert in Church law</note> on the Decretals of Gregory IX (which he named the *Novellae* in honor of his mother and daughter), and also in his so-called *Mercuriales* or *Inquiries into the Rules of Law*, nothing appears concerning this legal opinion <note>original: "Consilio"</note>.

<meta>This page continues the 14th-century alchemical recipe attributed to Giacomo of Arpino, transitioning from a list of ingredients to the process of melting and purification. It then moves into a historical discussion of Nicolas Flamel’s legendary alchemical associations in Paris.</meta>

Of saltpeter, 18 ounces. Of white borax <note>original: "Sorecara blanca," likely a corruption of "borace bianca."</note>, 18 ounces.

Of rock alum, 1 ounce.

*Also*, whatever thing you wish to pulverize, let it be the finest of the others, and then you must mix them together (*mix together*). *Also*, whatever thing is to enter into a pot, the pot must be well sealed with <term>lute of wisdom</term> <note>original: "lutu sapientia" (lutum sapientiae). A specialized alchemical paste made of clay, dung, and hair, used to seal vessels airtight so they could withstand high heat.</note> and covered so that nothing escapes, and then set it for 7 hours. *Also*, after it has been melted, it must be drenched in <term>oil of tartar</term> <note>original: "olu de tartaro." A concentrated solution of potassium carbonate, highly regarded by alchemists as a solvent or "liquor."</note>. *Also*, then take a crucible and put one <term>solaro</term> <note>original: "solaro." Likely referring to a "sol," a gold coin or a specific weight associated with the sun (gold).</note> into the crucible (*crucible*), and the copper must be purified.

*Also*, after it is uncovered, throw a little <term>alkali salt</term> <note>original: "sale argali," likely referring to salt of alkali or potash.</note> into the channel.

The hieroglyphic figures of <term>gold-making</term> <note>Alchemy or transmutation.</note> attributed to **Nicolas Flamel** <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418) was a successful French scrivener. Long after his death, legends claimed he had discovered the Philosopher's Stone and achieved immortality.</note> by tellers of fables are well known. One could see them on the fourth **arched gallery** of the great former **Cemetery of the Innocents**, to the right hand of the **Gate of Saint-Denis**. I will quote a remarkable passage regarding this from a very rare book by **Naudé** <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a famous French scholar and librarian to Cardinal Mazarin.</note>.

In his work *Judgment of Everything Printed Against Cardinal Mazarin; or, Le Mascurat* (1649, quarto edition, p. 261, and p. 340 of the second expanded edition), he wrote of **Flamel** thus:

**Saint-Ange.** (Bookseller) Have you ever seen the figures and emblems of **Flamel** beneath the charnel houses of **St. Innocent**?

**Mascurat.** (Printer) One would have to have never passed through there, or have no eyes at all, to have not seen them.

**S.** Well, what do you think of them?

<meta>This page transitions from the specific ingredients of a 14th-century recipe to a historical critique of Arnald of Villanova, a famous physician and alchemist. It references legal and biographical works from the 14th to 16th centuries to evaluate the legitimacy of medieval "gold-making."</meta>

<note>The text begins with a legal reference to the crime of fraud or "stellionate."</note> *In our days,* <note>original Latin: "Nostris diebus..." This quote is attributed to the 14th-century legal scholar Giovanni d'Andrea.</note> *we had Master Arnold of Villanova at the Roman Court, a supreme physician and theologian, about whom I wrote a legal opinion regarding the observation of fasts. He was also a very great alchemist; the little rods of gold that he made, he allowed to be submitted to every test.*

**Symphorian Campegius** (or **Champier**), in his biography of **Arnald of Villanova**, which is printed as a preface to Arnald's works (Venice 1505; Lyon 1509, 1520, and 1532; and Basel 1581 and 1585, folio), does not think much of Arnald’s gold-making. *"However,"* he writes, <note>original Latin: "Quae autem... de Alchemia Arnoldi dicuntur, falsa et plena fabularum sunt, et tanto viro indigna."</note> *"those things which are said regarding Arnald’s alchemy are false and full of fables, and unworthy of such a great man."* And what, then, were these small gold rods or gold plates? They were either heavily gilded iron or copper, or false gold that would never have withstood the <term>cupel</term> <note>original: "Kapelle." Referring to cupellation, a traditional refining process where ores are treated under high heat to separate noble metals like gold and silver from base metals.</note>.

And how did the gold-making recipes of the 14th century look in general? Here is one. *From the claw, the lion!* <note>Latin: "Ex ungue leonem" — a proverb suggesting that one can judge the nature of the whole thing from a small sample.</note>

### Manuscript of the 14th Century.

->Recipe given by Giacomo of Arpino.<-

->Take two ounces of borax and two ounces of <term>sublimated mercury</term>.<-
<note>original: "Argentum sublimatum." While "argentum" usually means silver, in alchemical recipes "argentum vivum" (living silver) refers to mercury; sublimated mercury was a common ingredient in transmutation experiments.</note>

---

\*) i.e., regarding the observation of fasts; Book III of the Decretals, title 46. In the commentary of **Giovanni d’Andrea** on the Decretals of Gregory IX (which he named the **Novellae** in honor of his mother and daughter), and also in his so-called *Mercuriales* or *Questions on the Rules of Law*, nothing regarding this legal opinion (*Consilio*) appears.

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of 14th-century alchemy. Having just introduced the skepticism surrounding Arnald of Villanova, the text now provides a specific recipe from that era before transitioning to the famous legend of Nicholas Flamel and his "hieroglyphic" symbols in Paris.</meta>

Of saltpeter, 19 ounces. White soda <note>original: "Sorecara blanca." Likely referring to a type of alkaline salt or purified tartar.</note>, 19 ounces.
Rock alum, 1 ounce.

Also, whatever thing you wish to pulverize, let it be done <note>original: "affimori lumi di lautro" — an obscure dialect phrase likely meaning to the fineness of the other alums</note> and then it must be mixed together (*mixed together*). Also, whatever thing must go into a pot, and the pot must be well-sealed with the **lute of wisdom** <term>lute of wisdom</term> <note>original: "lutu sapientia." A specialized hermetic sealant made of clay, dung, and hair used to airtight-seal alchemical vessels.</note> and covered so that nothing escapes, and then set for 7 hours. Also, after it has been fused, you should quench it in oil of tartar. Also then take a crucible and put a layer in the crucible (*crucible*) and there must be one ounce of the powder; the copper must be purified. Also, once uncovered, throw a bit of argol salt <note>original: "sale argali," referring to crude cream of tartar</note> into the spout.

The hieroglyphic figures of gold-making attributed to **Nicholas Flamel** by fabricators are well known. They were seen on the fourth arch of the large, former Cemetery of the Innocents, to the right of the St. Denis gate. I cite a remarkable passage concerning this from a very rare book by **Naudé**.

He wrote in the *Judgment of everything that has been printed against Cardinal Mazarin, or Le Mascurat* (1649, quarto, p. 261 and p. 340 of the second expanded edition) regarding Flamel:

> **Saint-Ange** (Bookseller): Have you ever seen the figures and emblems of Flamel under the charnel houses of St. Innocent? <note>original French: "As-tu jamais vu les figures et emblêmes de Flamel sous les Charniers de S. Innocent?"</note>
>
> **Mascurat** (Printer): One would have to have never passed through there, or have no eyes at all, not to have seen them. <note>original French: "Il faudroit n'avoir jamais passé par là, ou n'avoir point d'yeux pour ne les avoir pas vûs."</note>
>
> **S**: Well, what do you think of them? <note>original French: "Hé bien que t'en semble-t-il?"</note>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue from Gabriel Naudé’s work (writing under the pseudonym Mascurat), moving from a description of Nicholas Flamel’s legendary alchemical symbols to a historical discussion of his actual profession as a manuscript copyist and scribe.</meta>

**M.** That the secret of the **philosopher’s stone** <term>philosopher’s stone</term> <note>The legendary alchemical substance said to be capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> is just as well hidden beneath these grotesques and little figures <note>original: "grôtesques et marmousets." Fanciful, often distorted carvings of humans or animals common in Gothic architecture and manuscript margins.</note> as it is beneath the *Apocalypse*, the *Odyssey*, the *Metamorphoses*, and the *History of Theagenes and Chariclea* <note>A 3rd-century Greek novel by Heliodorus of Emesa. Alchemists often interpreted classical literature as encoded chemical recipes.</note>, where nevertheless these dreamers go looking for it. **Flammel** was a scribe in Paris who copied manuscripts for many **Lords**, and I remember having seen two very large ones—namely, a *Collection of Various Histories* and a *Romance of the Rose*. The first is kept in the **King's Library**, where **Messieurs du Puy**, that noble pair of brothers <note>original: "par illud nobile fratrum." Referring to Pierre and Jacques Dupuy, the renowned scholars and keepers of the Royal Library in the 17th century.</note>, took the trouble to show it to me. I saw the other in Rome in the study of a Cardinal, which he had obtained from the **Palace of Avignon** along with many other manuscripts when he was **Vice-Legate** *).

**S.** How could you know that these two manuscripts were by the hand of **Flammel** rather than someone else?

**M.** It is because he placed his name at the end, interlaced with various flourishes <note>original: "cadeaux." Elaborate calligraphic pen-strokes used by master scribes to decorate their signatures and prevent forgery.</note>, as is the custom of all good craftsmen.

**S.** And if some other writer of that time had done,

> As certain craftsmen do in our age,
> Who find a greater price for their works,
> If they have inscribed "Praxiteles" on new marble,
> Or "Myron" on their worn silver.
> <note>original Latin: "Ut quidam artifices nostro faciunt saeculo..." a quote from the Roman fabulist Phaedrus. The speaker suggests that Flamel's signature might have been forged on older works to increase their market value, much like ancient forgers used the names of famous Greek sculptors.</note>

->***Phaedrus***<-

---

*) It is well known that many books were collected under **Charles V** and **VI**; see the *History of the Academy, Volume I.* <note>original German: "Es ist bekannt, daß unter Karl V. und VI. viele Bücher gesammelt wurden..." This refers to the foundation of the French royal library collections.</note>

**S.** What would you say to having been so deceived, you who make a profession of never letting yourself be caught out by anyone?

**M.** I would admit the debt <note>An idiomatic expression meaning "I would concede the point."</note>, but I cannot have been mistaken about these manuscripts of **Flammel**, for they are very well written, and it is unlikely that the one who made them would have needed to gain an advantage or seek credit under the name of **Flammel**, since his own name would have been no less known or esteemed. Now, because this **Flammel** was also a broker for the Jews, and because he managed the lending of large sums of money for them to many private individuals, it happened that **Philip Augustus** <note>Philip II of France (reigned 1180–1223). The author is likely conflating the expulsions of the Jews under Philip Augustus with those that occurred during Flamel's actual lifetime in the 14th century, such as in 1306 or 1394.</note> having driven them out of France and confiscated all their property, as **William the Breton** <note>original: "Guillelmus Brito." A chronicler and chaplain to Philip Augustus.</note> remarks in his biography:

> *Without delay, he drove them from the borders of the entire kingdom,*
> *And purged the realm of its harmful decay;*
> *Yet he granted the wretched ones a suitable time in which*
> *To prepare themselves and their belongings for the journey, as custom required.*
> <note>original Latin: "Nec mora, de Regni totius abegit eosdem / Finibus, et nocua regnum putredine soluit, / Indulto miseris tamen apto tempore, quo res / Seque suaque parant ad iter, prout exigit usus."</note>

**Flammel** settled very profitably with the aforementioned individuals regarding the sums they owed to the Jews, because otherwise he threatened to make them lose everything by reporting them to the courts. And by this means, he became so rich in a short time that those who did not know the cause, and who perhaps knew **Flammel** as a **puffer** <term>puffer</term> <note>original: "souffleur" (bellows-blower). A derogatory term for "vulgar" alchemists who focused on the physical heat of the furnace rather than the spiritual or philosophical "Great Work."</note>, had reason to believe for certain that he had found the **philosopher's stone** <term>philosopher's stone</term> <note>The legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note>.

whatever metal she sprinkled it over while heating it,
the powder then entered the mass of the body,

metals—or rather, to seek to transform them—in order
to deceive the gullible.

Moreover, I saw many trickeries *) and deceptions from her; I corrected her with words. She, however, wanted to imprison me; but I departed from her in peace, because God helped me.

<meta>This page continues the document introduced on the previous page: the "Solemn Oath of Submission" of Heinrich von Freyberg, dated 1447. I, Heinrich von Freiberg of Waule, who calls himself a knight, publicly acknowledge with this letter: whereas I acted against the high-born princess and lady, Lady Margaret of Brandenburg, etc., my gracious lady, in violation of my oath and vow, and wished to have her matched <note>original: "verkuppelt" — here referring to an unauthorized or scandalous attempt to arrange a marriage or liaison for a noblewoman.</note> with Count Hanns; since the pious princess and also the pious Count did not wish to do this, on account of such wickedness and misdeed, which I have committed against my gracious Lord Margrave Albrecht, and have thereby become faithless and perjured, for which his grace took me into custody and I would have justly deserved punishment to my body and life <note>A legal euphemism for the death penalty.</note>; I have, through the pleas of my friends, and also ladies and men, who looked upon my insanity <note>original: "Vnsynn" — madness or mental instability. Heinrich uses this "insanity plea" as a legal strategy to escape execution for his treasonous behavior.</note>, as I—alas—became of unsound mind *) <note>original: "mennysch." This term likely refers to a manic state, though the editor's footnote provides a different interpretation.</note>, in which insanity I also forfeited my paternal inheritance—those who have prayed for me for God's sake—accordingly, I give myself to his grace as his own <note>This implies a form of total submission or voluntary bondage, where the individual becomes the legal property of the lord to avoid harsher punishment.</note> and, by virtue of this letter and the oath I have sworn to his grace, I surrender myself nevermore to act against him or any of his associates, whether spiritual or worldly. Also, I shall teach all my art without gift or fee, and [place] the art of alchemy at his grace's [disposal] at my own [expense]...

---

Lexikon."</note> Part II, page 363, a Heinrich von Freyberg appears, but he is said to have lived around the year 1260. One really does not know whether this Heinrich was considered a fool (this seems likely to me) or if the matter was meant seriously.

Illustrious, High-born Prince, Gracious Lord, I make known to your Princely Grace that there has come to me a pious, good, adventurous <note>original: "abenteuerlicher" — in this context, the word implies a seeker of curiosities, secrets, or wonders.</note> fellow who has identified himself to me and has offered to perform a secret art <term>art <note>original: "Kunst" — in the 15th century, this often referred to alchemy, mechanical inventions, or hidden technical skills.</note></term> for me that he claims to know. He said that if I knew a Prince who was adventurous *) in spirit, he would teach it to him, provided that he were rewarded with fair and just compensation. Gracious Lord, upon this I have resolved to write to your Grace, and I pray your Grace will not take this writing in bad favor, seeing as your Grace has no personal acquaintance with me; for I do this for no other reason than because, as long as I have been a man, I have heard over and over again that your Grace is the most adventurous Prince currently living. Gracious Lord, for this reason I have written to your Grace and now disclose to your Grace what this art is. Gracious Lord, if your Grace intends to pursue such an art and finds it pleasing, let your Grace inform me of this by means of these messengers <term>messengers <note>original: "Potten" — professional couriers used for diplomatic or private correspondence.</note></term>. If this then pleases your Grace, I will then bring the man with me to your Grace, so that your Grace shall then become aware of the truth, that the

Also, gracious Lord, you must have a goldsmith for these matters at the beginning, for as long as fourteen days; thereafter you will need him no longer. May your Grace know how to proceed accordingly.

In a parchment manuscript in 24mo format <note>A very small "pocket" size book where the paper is folded to create 24 leaves per sheet.</note> which bears the inscription: *Recipes of the Great Basilius Valentinus. Dated at Erfurt, in the year of our Lord 1498, on the very day of the Holy Apostles Simon and Jude.* <note>October 28, 1498. Basilius Valentinus is a legendary alchemical figure, traditionally a Benedictine monk in Erfurt, though many scholars now believe these writings were actually authored in the early 1600s.</note> The first recipe bears the heading: *Recipe for vermilion to extract from a pound of twelve ounces six lots <term>lots <note>original: "lotones" / "Loth" — a pre-metric unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 1/30th to 1/32nd of a pound (approx. 15 grams).</note></term> of the best silver*, with the testimony: *I, Jo. Casp. Thorer of Baden, possessed this in the year [15]25.*

Basilius Valentinus was already familiar with the <term>antimony tincture <note>original: "Spiesglanztinktur" — a medicinal or alchemical preparation made from antimony, highly regarded in early modern chemistry.</note></term> so highly recommended by the late Theden. <note>Johann Christian Anton Theden (1714–1797) was a famous Prussian surgeon-general who promoted chemical remedies.</note>

Johannes de Colonia <note>John of Cologne.</note> says that there were only three true Adepts <note>An "Adept" is an alchemist who has successfully discovered the "Philosopher's Stone" or the secret of transmutation.</note> in Germany from the year 1480 to 1520. They were unknown to Semler. <note>Johann Salomo Semler (1725–1791), a famous theologian who also wrote a history of alchemy.</note>

1) Magister Burckhard, born in Kreuzberg, a place four miles from Giessen. He possessed both the red and the white tincture. <note>In alchemy, the "white tincture" was said to transmute metals into silver, while the "red tincture" (the Philosopher's Stone) transmuted them into gold.</note>

2) Georg Krapit, a servant of the Elector of Trier. With one lot of tincture, he transformed 600 lots of mercury into good silver.

3) Ludwig von Neis, a Silesian **). He lived in Marburg with Johann von Dorrenberg, who was the court master <note>A high-ranking administrative official or "Hofmeister."</note> to Landgraves Heinrich and Wilhelm. With one ounce of tincture, he frequently transformed 16 ounces of mercury into just as 

---

*) The third report of the Superintendent G. M. Schnizer on the Church Library at Neustadt an der Aisch, 1784. 4. p. 29.

**) *The Chemistry of Geber the Arab, or, The Tradition of the Sum of Perfection, with a preface by G. Horn. Leiden, 1668. 12mo. in the Preface.* <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, the foundational figure of Islamic and European alchemy.</note>

...much fine 24-karat gold in the presence of many people. ## 16th Century.

### History: The Story of Emperor Maximilian, of glorious memory, with the Alchemist.

->When, fifty-three years ago,<-
->In my youth I wished to learn much,<-
->As I traveled for my trade<-
->From one city to another,<-
->I also came into the city of Wels, <note>A city in Upper Austria.</note><-
->Where the river Traun flows by,<-
->Where Emperor Maximilian,<-
->The great, powerful, and noble man,<-
->Had held court in times past,<-
->Who was well-versed in courtly manners,<-
->With jousting, tilting, and racing,<-
->And whatever one might call pastime,<-
->Such as fencing, shooting, piping, hunting,<-
->Climbing after chamois in his day.<-
->To these he gave his joy and favor,<-
->As well as to ingenious, subtle art<-

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic account by Hans Sachs regarding Emperor Maximilian I. The previous page established the setting in the city of Wels and described the Emperor’s love for both courtly arts and subtle sciences. This page begins the narrative of a specific encounter with an alchemist.</meta>

To the court there came many a guest,
Who were treated in the best manner,
Noble, common, and skillful folk,
In whom the Emperor took his delight.
It is said that one day
An alchemist came to the court
In peasant’s clothing, quite coarse in form,
Who, defiantly and as if by force,
Wished to enter the Emperor’s chamber
Wherein Emperor Maximilian
Was holding council with his advisors.
As he now stepped to the hall's gate,
The doorkeeper addressed him boldly,
Asking what business he had within.
The artist <note>The text refers to the alchemist as a "Künstler" (artist), a common term for those practicing the "Hermetic Art" of alchemy.</note> gave a defiant answer:
"I have business with the Emperor."
The gatekeeper said: "Be off <note>original: *fetsch*. An archaic dialect command meaning 'get lost' or 'clear out.' The original text notes a linguistic curiosity that it sounds like the English word 'fetch,' though the meaning is different.</note> on your way,
In short, I will not let you in;
The Emperor has other business
Than to listen to your babble,"
For he took him for a mere vagrant, <note>original: *Laufkind*, literally 'running child,' used here to describe a low-status errand-runner or beggar.</note>
And pushed him quickly from the door.
The alchemist became indignant,
The doorkeeper accosted him again,
They both became loud and rowdy,
Until the quarreling in the hall
Was heard by the Emperor, who sent his herald
To command that the man be let in,
Who so desired <note>original: *gert*, an abbreviation of *begehret*.</note> to see the Emperor.

First they let this artist <note>original: *Künstner*. In this period, "artist" frequently referred to those practicing the "Hermetic Art" of alchemy.</note> in
In his coarse peasant's clothing,
Unknown to all the courtly household,
He stepped toward the Emperor in haste,
Without any pomp or reverence,
And to the Emperor he did say:

> Emperor, do you wish to see from me here
> The true, artful foundations of alchemy, <term>Alchemy: The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on the transformation of matter, particularly the transmutation of base metals into gold.</term>
> Of which I am a free master,
> To make clear gold out of copper?

The Emperor replied to these matters:

> Yes, I have ever held favor
> Toward alchemy, the free art;
> If you can do it, I shall help you toward it;
> State what you require for it?
> If you succeed without any deceit or tricks,
> You shall receive an imperial gift.

The alchemist spoke to the Emperor:

> Give me an empty room in the court,
> And give me one mark of pure gold, <note>original: *lötigs Goldt*. This refers to gold of a certified purity, measured by the *Lot* system.</note>
> Nine marks <term>Mark: A traditional unit of weight for precious metals, common in the Holy Roman Empire, weighing approximately 230 grams.</term> of copper you should also give,
> Coal, bellows, crucible, and tongs;
> Bring to me mercury and salt,
> Glassware, pots, sulfur, and hearth-stone;
> Have a furnace <note>original: *Camin*. While literally a chimney, here it refers to the specialized furnace or "athanor" used by alchemists.</note> built therein,
> In which I may melt and distill,
> And prepare <note>original: *conficir*, from the Latin *conficere*, meaning to compose or complete a chemical preparation.</note> the matter with art;
> In a month’s time you may
> Come down to me from your hall,
> And behold my artful work,
> Which I will make for you in good faith,
> Such as you have never from any guest [seen before]...

...seen so thoroughly,
Otherwise, I will allow
No one else to go in and out to me.
The Emperor <term>Emperor Maximilian: Maximilian I (1459–1519), Holy Roman Emperor, was a famous patron of the arts and sciences who maintained a lifelong interest in alchemy and the "Great Work."</term> took great pleasure

Carrying out his artful work

Then you shall see it even more clearly
And only then give my art its true praise."

Joyful and pondering the art—

The artist had secretly

Went down to the court, into the artist's chamber,

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of the mysterious alchemist who worked for Emperor Maximilian I. On the previous page, the artist had fled his chamber in the middle of the night, leaving the Emperor to discover what remained.</meta>

In there [he] saw the artist no more,
Yet he saw a golden cake
Lying on the table, weighing ten marks, <term>ten marks: A "mark" was a traditional unit of weight for precious metals, roughly equivalent to 230 grams; ten marks would be a substantial mass of gold, weighing about 5 pounds.</term>
Of pure, good, clear gold,
Upon which it was written thus:
"O Emperor Maximilian,
He who possesses these arts
Looks neither to you nor the Roman Empire
To come to you for favor." <note>This note suggests that a true master of alchemy is self-sufficiently wealthy and therefore beyond the control of even the most powerful monarchs.</note>

When the Emperor read the writing,
He understood quite clearly that
The alchemist and foreign artist
Had been a Venetian,
With whom the Emperor was at odds; <note>The Republic of Venice was a frequent political and military rival of Maximilian I during the Italian Wars.</note>
Therefore, Maximilian ordered
The alchemist to be sought
By water and by land, yet he was not found—
He who had gifted the Emperor
This banquet <note>original: *Panket*. Here used metaphorically for the miraculous display of alchemical success or the "feast" of gold left behind.</note> of alchemy.
Thus was the legend told entirely
In Wels, <note>Wels: A city in Upper Austria. The narrator mentions hearing this story while working there as a journeyman or craftsman.</note> as I heard in my day
While I worked at my trade there;
Only a few years afterward,
The Emperor returned to Wels,
Where he met a blessed end. <note>Emperor Maximilian I died in Wels on January 12, 1519.</note>

This was either a folk legend or a similar occurrence, only it was more significant than what happened to Dr. Schweitzer—or <term>Helvetius: Johann Friedrich Schweitzer (1630–1709), a famous physician and alchemist who recorded a famous instance of transmutation in 1666 after being visited by a mysterious stranger.</term>—which will be discussed further below.

<meta>This page begins a new section following the anecdotal history of Emperor Maximilian I. It transitions from local legends to a famous allegorical text, "The Dialogue of Mercury, the Alchemist, and Nature," which was a popular pedagogical story used to explain alchemical theory.</meta>

## Dialogue of Mercury, the Alchemist,
## and Nature,

In that time, the Chymists <note>original: *Chymistae*. At this time, the words "chemist" and "alchemist" were often used interchangeably to describe those who studied the transformations of matter.</note> gathered together and held a council on how they ought to make and prepare the PHILOSOPHERS' STONE, and thus they decided that each man should state his own opinion by vote. And that gathering took place in the open air in a certain meadow on a certain clear day. And many agreed that Mercury <note>The symbol ☿ represents the element and planetary spirit of Mercury.</note> was its first matter <term>first matter: known as *materia prima*, this was the primitive, formless base of all substance that alchemists sought to isolate.</term>, others Venus <note>The symbol ♀ represents copper, associated with the planet Venus.</note>, others other things. But regarding Mercury, the greatest opinion was among them, especially from the sayings of the Philosophers, since they hold it to be the true first matter, and even the first matter of Metals: for the Philosophers cry out: **OUR MERCURY**. <note>original: *NOSTER MERCURIUS*. Alchemists often distinguished between "common mercury" (quicksilver) and "philosophical mercury," a purified or secret substance.</note> And while they were thus contending among themselves with various works (for each of them waited with desire and joy for the conclusion), a great storm arose meanwhile with gales, clouds, and an unheard-of wind, which dispersed that gathering into various provinces, leaving each man on his own without a conclusion. Yet each of them impressed upon himself what the end of that dispute ought to have been. Therefore, each began his labors as before, one seeking the PHILOSOPHERS' STONE in this thing, another in that; and this happens even until now without ceasing. But one of them, mindful of the dispute—that the Philosophers' Stone must necessarily be sought in Mercury—said to himself: "Even if a conclusion was not reached, I will nonetheless work in Mercury, and I myself shall reach the conclusion by making the blessed stone." For he was a man who always used to talk to himself, as Alchemists are wont to do. Therefore, he began to read the books of the Philosophers, and he came upon a book...

<summary>This page introduces an allegorical dialogue where a group of alchemists fails to reach a consensus on the true "first matter" of the Philosophers' Stone before being scattered by a storm. One persistent alchemist decides to pursue the secret through Mercury, following the traditional authorities of the field.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophers' Stone, Mercury, first matter, alchemy council, allegory, chemical symbols</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the allegorical "Dialogue of Mercury," moving from the general gathering of alchemists to the specific, comical failures of a single "Alchemist-Philosopher." It then shifts abruptly from the story to a historical account of the Duke of Saxony's alchemical interests during his imprisonment.</meta>

...of Alanus <note>original: *Alani*. Likely referring to Alain de Lille (c. 1128–1202), a French theologian and poet to whom several alchemical works were falsely attributed.</note>, who treats of Mercury, and so that **Alchemist-Philosopher** was made, though without reaching a conclusion. And having taken Mercury <note>The symbol ☿ represents Mercury or quicksilver.</note>, he began to work; he placed it in a glass vessel and applied fire. Mercury, as is its custom, evaporated. The wretched Alchemist, ignorant of the nature of things, beat his wife, saying: "No one could have come in here except for you; you have taken my Mercury out of the glass!" The wife, weeping, excuses herself, and says quietly to her husband: "You will make nothing but dung <note>original: *merdam*</note> out of these things." 

The Alchemist took Mercury once more, and again placed it in a vessel, and lest his wife should take it, he guarded it himself. But Mercury, in its own way, evaporated again. The Alchemist, remembering that the first matter <term>first matter: the *materia prima*, the pure, undifferentiated substance from which all metals were thought to originate.</term> of the **Philosophers' Stone** must be volatile, rejoiced greatly. Persuading himself entirely that he could no longer be deceived now that he possessed this material, he began to treat Mercury boldly. He learned to sublime it and calcine it in wondrous ways—now with salt <note>The symbol ☐ in this context refers to salt or mineral salts.</note>, now with copper <note>The symbol ♀ represents Venus or copper.</note>, now with metals, now with minerals, now with blood, now with hair, now with strong waters <note>The symbol ▽ refers to water; *▽ fortibus* is *aqua fortis* or nitric acid, used to dissolve metals.</note>, now with herbs, urine, and vinegar. 

But he could find nothing to suit his purpose; he left nothing in the world untouched with which he had not treated the good Mercury. And when he could profit nothing at all, he came upon this saying: "it is found in the dung-heap." <note>A common alchemical paradox: the "Stone" or the "First Matter" is often described as a vile, cheap thing found everywhere, even in filth.</note> He began to treat Mercury with various types of dung, both together and separately. And when he had been thus driven about and was full of heavy thoughts, he fell asleep.

***

The unfortunate Duke of Saxony, **Johann Friedrich the Middle**, corresponded during his imprisonment regarding chemical matters with a gold-refiner named Raschberger in Vienna; with the court preacher of Henneberg, Scherdiger; and with the Doctor of Medicine, Stadmion von der Matt at...

In the year 1575, there was an alchemical fraudster in Nuremberg who called himself Count Hieronymus Scoto, and who committed great deceptions in Cologne and Coburg. He was still active in Germany in 1593. He had so ingratiated himself with the founder of the famous former art collection, Mr. Paul von Praun, as early as 1582, that Hanns Hofmann <note>A German painter (c. 1530–1591/92) known for his copies of Dürer and portraits of the Nuremberg elite.</note> was commissioned to paint him three times. In 1590, he even had the honor of dining with the Margrave of Onoldsbach <note>Ansbach</note>, Johann Friedrich, in Roth. ## II.

# L e t t e r s

From original letters of good chemists to the Senior Mine Administrator, Franz Kretschmer at Goldkronach. From the Plassenburg Archives. *)

->Oswald Croll.<-

To Master Franz Kretschmer, Mine Master at Goldkronach on the Fichtelberg etc.
To his singular friend.

->Goldkronach.<-
->Regensburg, September 7, 1594.<-

->I.<-

— Regarding Korndorffer’s process <note>Bartholomäus Korndorffer was a 16th-century alchemist known for his supposed transmutational recipes</note> about which you write, concerning <term>Jupiter</term> <note>original: "♃", the alchemical symbol for the planet Jupiter, which represents the metal tin</note>, I have none. Mr. Heuser once gave me many <term>particulars</term> <note>In alchemy, a "particular" is a recipe intended to transmute a specific base metal into a small amount of gold or silver, rather than the "Universal" work of the Philosopher's Stone</note> to read here, which were from Korndorffer's manuscripts, but I returned them to him and copied nothing down, as it is most certain that *no true particular exists from which the Universal <note>The "Universal" or "Great Work" refers to the creation of the Philosopher's Stone, which could transmute any metal and cure all diseases</note> does not arise*, or if there were some other way, the expenses would nevertheless exceed the profit. Let us be content, my Lord, with what we now have at hand. Believe me, it is an infallible path, if in this...

---

*) These letters were kindly shared with me by the late Mr. Senior Archivist Spieß.

...if we have proceeded correctly. I recommend to you (so that my affection might be more apparent) *Fernel* <note>Jean Fernel (1497–1558), a prominent French physician whose work "On the Hidden Causes of Things" discussed the occult properties of medicines and the Philosopher's Stone</note> on the hidden causes of things, where he discusses our Stone. Believe me (as an experienced friend writes thus to me), a grateful posterity will never be able to offer worthy thanks to *Fernel*, because he presents that mystery so openly to the whole world. Almost everything is to be understood literally. The matter, the form, the weight—in his method of operation, he differs from others, which is something that cannot hinder a practiced chemist <note>original: "chymicum"</note>. I rejoice greatly that you are girding yourself for the preparation of the Philosophers' Gold <note>original: "A. Philosophorum", likely standing for Aurum Philosophorum</note>. Since this is but a single labor, you could take care to make a good supply of F and G <note>These likely refer to specific materials or chemical reagents encoded by the author</note>. For I would desire to have one ounce <note>original symbol: "℥i"</note> of each. I will gladly pay the price.

Also, see that you do not neglect that convenience and kind generosity which your man offered to you of his own accord. That gift is worthy of you sending a particularly faithful man to him for that purpose, lest you later regret losing the opportunity, which is usually fleeting. I have translated for you word-for-word from the Italian into Latin an Enigma from *Ventura* <note>Lorenzo Ventura, a 16th-century Venetian alchemist and physician</note>.

### 2.

— In Nuremberg I have an Italian friend, *Cesare Calendrini*, in the *Stöpfelgeßlein* <note>A small alleyway in Nuremberg</note>. If letters were delivered to him through your man *Lorenzo Zazero*, he could safely forward them to me. For first and foremost I wish to know whether you are seeking further regarding the Gold-matter <note>original: "$\odot$rio", using the astrological symbol for the Sun to represent Gold</note> and 그 mineral, so that I may provide for it in time. Let me know, as [I am dealing with] that treatise of *Hoghelande* <note>Theobald van Hoghelande (c. 1560–1608), a Flemish alchemist who wrote in defense of the art</note> on Al[chemy]—

---

> They know the secret arts, to extract the blood
> By a technique second to none.
> And because within it there is Mercury and sulfur,
> Gold and Silver—not those of the common sort,
> But a celestial humor <note>A vital fluid or moisture</note>, hot and oily,
> With its subtle earthiness, cold and dry,
> Bound together in a marvelous way:
> Soul, spirit, and body—four and one.
> Here, with the gift of God, goodness, and patience,
> Toil, diligence, fire, and long time,
> The substance alone rises from the previously taken
> Impure body, and makes it clear and bright.
> The art darkens and clears them again
> Many times, until the light and heavy, the rare and dense,
> Are well united and exposed
> To the great martyrdom of Vulcan <note>The alchemical fire</note>:
> This is called the Elixir and the medicine
> That heals every "leper" <note>In alchemy, "leprosy" refers to the impurities in base metals</note> and imperfect
> Metal, and the human body, to wonder.
> This makes man rich, it makes him happy.

### 3.

->4.<-

— Regarding the Englishman **Chyleus** (*Kelley*), I promise myself nothing good; I fear he may lead the **Emperor** by the nose <note>original: "naso suspendat," a Latin idiom for mocking or deceiving someone</note>. I saw the powder of *Lavinius*, and although I once shared a familiarity with him in **France** and knew that he was devoted to chemical preparations, I could not have suspected that he was dealing with the transmutation of metals. You must act cautiously with that powder, lest—as our friend *Hiller* reported to me—you waste your labor and coals, or your oil.

...to give a lead. He was just with the Englishman (**Kelley**); he has him involved in the work, and if it proceeds correctly, I intend to carry it out. He wanted a sum of money for it, however. It is said to yield much <term>mercury</term> <note>original symbol: ☿</note>. It would be worth trying with a small amount.

### 7.
## Lorenz Sazer.

— The great <term>Universal Medicine</term> <note>original: "Medicina Panacea"</note> called <term>Georg Waldica</term> <note>Likely referring to the "Panacea Amwaldina," a famous and controversial universal remedy sold by the physician Georg am Wald</note> is (as I know for a fact) made from <term>cinnabar</term> or <term>mercury ore</term> along with other additives. <meta>This page continues the correspondence from Lorenz Sazer in Nuremberg, following his critical remarks about the "Medicina Panacea" on the previous page. He now describes a specific alchemical experiment involving silver and mercury, followed by the start of a new entry from Thomas Hiller, a physician in Ansbach.</meta>

### 8.

— Whether one works in the <term>dissolution</term> <note>original Latin: "Solutio"</note> of **gold** <note>symbol: ☉</note> or **silver** <note>symbol: ☽</note> with vegetable, animal, or mineral <term>spirits</term> <note>volatile liquids produced by distillation</note> or water, it is all in vain if these are not combined with the <term>philosophical mercury</term> <note>the "mercurio" of the alchemists, considered the essential spirit of metals</note> itself. I once took the strong mineral **nitric acid** <note>symbol: ≈, original Latin: "aqua fortis"</note> and dissolved a small amount of **mercury** <note>symbol: ☿rii</note> in it, and then dissolved 1 <term>loth</term> <note>a German unit of weight, approximately 14–17 grams</note> of **silver** <note>symbol: ☽</note> with it. After it had stood for half an hour in moderate heat, the **silver** settled to the bottom, looking just like a mountain in the water; and when the glass was swirled with the matter, it was living **quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "**arg. viuum**" / argentum vivum</note>. I poured off the water and pressed the **quicksilver** through a piece of leather. Most of it passed through, and only a small part remained in the leather as an <term>amalgam</term> <note>a blend of mercury with another metal</note>; when this amalgam was placed in a <term>crucible</term> <note>original: "Tigel"</note> over coals, almost all of it smoked away, and only a tiny bit of metal remained. This flowed in moderate heat like lead, and was as soft to cut as **lead** <note>symbol: ♄, "Saturn"</note> or **tin** <note>symbol: ♃, "Jupiter"</note>. However, because the correct preparation of the **mercury** remains hidden from me, and I do not know how to perfectly extract and prepare it, I cannot make any further progress for that reason.
— Nuremberg, the 13th of April 1596.

->Lorenz Sazer, by his own hand<-
<note>original Latin: "m. p." / manu propria</note>
