## 10.

## Konrad Scherer 1595.

— Concerning the process given to me by a good friend; First, the <term>Regulus of Antimony</term> <note>the pure metallic form of antimony</note> should be taken, very well purified; afterward it should be <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to powder by heat or chemicals</note> through the **water** <note>symbol: ∇</note> of **salt** <note>symbol: ⦵</note>. This same <term>calx</term> <note>powdery residue</note> should be well <term>imbibed</term> <note>thoroughly soaked or saturated</note> with **oil of Tartar** <note>a concentrated solution of potassium carbonate</note> and prepared **sal ammoniac** <note>ammonium chloride</note>, and placed in a gentle heat until it becomes gray-black. Then, when <term>driven up</term> <note>sublimated or distilled</note> in a <term>sand bath</term> <note>original: "Arena"</note>, it should yield a living **mercury** <note>quicksilver</note>. I have not yet attempted this, however. It proceeds similarly with **silver** <note>symbol: ☽</note>. When it is dissolved in **nitric acid** <note>original: "Aquafort"</note>, or in **salt** <note>symbol: ⦵</note> **water** <note>symbol: ∇</note> and <term>drawn off</term> <note>distilled away</note> in a **water bath** <note>original: "Balneo"</note> until it reaches an oily consistency. Afterward, <term>imbibe</term> it with **oil of Tartar** and **sal ammoniac**, and let it <term>digest</term> <note>be kept in steady, gentle heat</note> for 10 days; and then, when <term>driven up</term> as mentioned above, a black powder will cling to the top of the <term>helm</term> <note>the cap or head of a still</note>. This should be removed and washed with a good vinegar, and it will yield a living **mercury**. I believe that if one proceeded with the **Regulus** in this way as well, it would be all the better. I have sublimated beautiful <term>flowers</term> <note>original: "Flores," the flaky crystals formed by sublimation</note> from the **Regulus**, crystalline and white, without any additives. Within it lies the true metallic form, and in my opinion, a high Ar- <note>likely "Arcanum," a secret or great medicinal mystery</note>

<summary>This page records alchemical observations and recipes from 1595, specifically focusing on the creation of "living mercury" through the chemical treatment of antimony and silver. It highlights the use of various substances like oil of tartar and sal ammoniac in the refinement process.</summary>

<keywords>antimony, Regulus, mercury, silver, sublimation, calcination, Thomas Hiller, Konrad Scherer, alchemical symbols, Ansbach</keywords>

## II.

### Anonymous 1596.

— Regarding the **antimony of gold** <note>symbols: ♁ ☉is</note>, I have indeed never been able to work on it myself, nor do I know if Duke Wilhelm has a correct process for it; however, an artist in Prague gave a process to my most gracious Lord, which His Electoral Grace has bestowed upon me, and which I send herewith to you in the hope that you will share something good with me in return. — When you have finished your **red gold** <note>symbol: ☉ rubrum; likely the "red lion" or a gold-based medicine</note> and your **tincture of silver** <note>symbol: ☽ii</note>, please share those as well. — Dated Glogau, July 21, [15]96.

->T. whose hand you know<- <note>original Latin: "cuius manum nosti," a common signature among friends to ensure privacy</note>

### Extracting **antimony** [and] **gold**.

Take: **Calx of silver** <note>symbol: ☽ae</note>, 4 <term>Lot</term> <note>a historical German unit of weight, roughly 15-16 grams</note>.
**Salt of urine** <note>ammonium carbonate or similar salts derived from urine</note>, 16 Lot.

Grind them together, put them in a flask and place a helm <note>an alembic or still-head</note> upon it in a sand bath. Let it stand for 24 hours in gentle heat; afterward, give it a stronger fire so that the salt of urine <term>sublimates</term> <note>turns to vapor and solidifies at the top of the vessel</note>. Pour onto the calx the spirit...

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the alchemical recipes for processing silver and urine salts from the previous page and begins a new section of correspondence involving the court of Emperor Rudolf II in 1596.</meta>

...rectified spirit of wine <note>alcohol distilled to high purity</note>, it extracts the soul <note>original Latin: *animam*; the essential essence or tincture</note> alone. Calcine the remains under a muffle furnace for several hours or more, and extract the salt with the strongest distilled vinegar <note>original Latin: *aceto destill. fortissimo*</note>; it becomes almost entirely salt.

Adding 4 <term>Lot</term> <note>a historical unit of weight, roughly 15-16 grams</note> of this salt to 1 <term>Mark</term> <note>a weight of approximately 230 grams</note> of gold <note>symbol: ☉</note> in flux <note>while the metal is melted</note> makes the gold as brittle as glass. Then, crush it and extract its soul with animated spirit of wine <note>original Latin: *Spiritu vini animato*; spirit of wine prepared with another substance to increase its solvent power</note>.

The calx of silver <note>symbol: ☽</note> should be filled with salt of urine <note>symbol: II</note>.

**Animated Spirit of Wine.** Calcine copper <note>symbol: ♀</note> until it is completely green <note>original Latin: *usque ad totalem viriditatem*</note>, then pour rectified spirit of wine over this copper; it will extract the redness. Distill the spirit of wine from the copper, and it rises up red; separate it again in a water bath <note>original Latin: *balneo*</note>, and it becomes white. This spirit extracts the soul of gold.

Take:
1 part soul of gold <note>symbol: ☉ri</note>.
2 parts crystals of Saturn <note>lead acetate</note>.

Rub them together, place them on driving lead <note>lead used in cupellation, a process for refining precious metals</note>, and you will have gold <note>symbol: ☉</note>.

### In the Name of Emperor Rudolf II (1596)

Noble, steadfast, favorable, and dear Mr. Kretschmair.

I have received your letter to me and to His Imperial Majesty and have presented it to His Majesty. In it, you mention that with Klette’s work <note>likely referring to a process by an alchemist named Klette</note>, after a long time, something might finally be achieved, though it is laborious until one reaches the stage of fixation <note>the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat</note> and the material is brought to a gray color <note>original Latin: *ad griseum Colorem*</note>. This work, however, could be greatly shortened, for one could extract the tincture from the gold, or [by taking] the whole...

to bring the <term>Body of the Sun</term> <note>original Latin: *Corpus Solis*; an alchemical name for gold</note> into an irreducible liquid <note>a stable liquid form that cannot easily be returned to solid metal</note>, or so that it could be easily used and made fluid. Since His Majesty now knew of or possessed a way to achieve the mentioned means, the matter could easily be assisted and progress toward a result. Thus His Majesty now fully believes that he has exactly what the Lord mentions, and holds that it is the true <term>essence of gold</term> <note>original Latin: *ens auri*; the "being" or essential core of gold</note>. It has such a quality that when one mixes it with common <term>mercury</term> <note>original symbol: ☿rio</note>, it not only prevents the mercury from rising <note>referring to sublimation or evaporation when heated</note>, but dissolves it into a completely clear water. Whether this is a good sign, His Majesty desires to know, as well as the process of how His Majesty should work within or with it. Hanns v. Heyden

<meta>This page continues the correspondence from the court of Emperor Rudolf II in 1596. It concludes a letter from an associate (likely Sebald Schwärzer) discussing his alchemical progress and his legal dispute with Edward Kelley, and begins a new report from an anonymous source regarding Kelley's imprisonment.</meta>

I have given my petition <note>original Latin: *supplicem libellum*; a formal written request or legal petition</note> against Kelley to His Majesty; I am waiting for a decision on it. ---

As for the *work of Bernard* <note>referring to the influential 15th-century alchemist Bernard of Trevisan</note>, I made an amalgam <note>a blend of metals</note> from leaves of gold <note>original: ☉</note> in the cold; however, I gradually heated it over a furnace in a heated room, so that my amalgam was not only sufficiently joined, but I could still press out the remaining part of the mercury <note>original: ☿ii</note>. After it had warmed up a little, I then pressed it with a wooden pestle in a crucible until it was fully joined. Then, at the same time, I broke the ball into small pieces, like grains of millet. Once the material was placed in glass, and having evaporated a little moisture at least in the heated room over the furnace, I sealed it; then I placed it in the furnace. The degree of fire was such that the mercury would not sublimate <note>turn into vapor and rise</note>, yet the glass vessel of material was hot enough; it then sat in the same furnace for several months. Finally, the whole material began to swell, and it was as if the surface were smeared with some kind of fatness; finally, when Kelley arrived, I began other things and left the rest. Farewell. Given at Prague, September 8th, New Style, in the year 1596.

->Your old<-
->friend<-

<meta>This page continues the catalog of famous (and often infamous) alchemists. **Marco Bragadino** \*), a native of Cyprus (he was commonly called Mamugna), claimed to be able to make gold from base metals. He made a great stir in Venice, producing a small quantity of gold from mercury in the house of the nobleman <note>original: *Nobile*</note> Contarini and worked in the Dandolo Palace, but was forced to flee Venetian territory due to the discovery of his villainy \*\*). He went to Munich, where he repeated his deceptions. In August 1590, he received his just reward <note>a euphemism for his execution</note> and was hanged in a garment covered with imitation gold <term>Lahngold: a form of flattened wire or tinsel made of copper or brass, used here to mock the alchemist's failure to produce real gold</term>, as were later his worthy successors Honauer and Müllenfels in Stuttgart, Krohnemann in Kulmbach, and Caetano in Küstrin. Two huge black bulldogs <term>Bullenbeißer: an extinct breed of powerful dogs, ancestors to the modern Boxer, often used for hunting or bull-baiting</term> that he took with him on his travels were shot at the same time. At that time, they were considered first-rate demons. De Villamont spoke with him and asserts that he really was able to produce gold \*\*\*).

In the year 1597, on the 2nd of April, Georg Honauer, Lord of Brunhof and Grobschiß <note>literally "Great-Shit"; a place name often cited in historical records to emphasize the ignominious end of this alchemist</note> from

---

\*) Andrea Maurocenus, *History of Venice* <note>original Latin: *Hist. Venetar.*</note>, Book XIV. Thuanus, Book 99, at the end; Part IV, page 884, Frankfurt edition, 1621, large octavo. Conring repeats both passages in Book II, chapter XI, *On Hermetic Medicine* <note>original Latin: *de hermetica Medicina*</note>, page 357 and following, 2nd edition.

\*\*) He presented the Senate with a transmuting powder, which much later the chemist Otto Tachen declared to be the work of a fraud.

\*\*\*) *Description of a Journey to Jerusalem* <note>original German: *Reisebeschr. nach Jerusalem*</note>, Book III, Chapter 18.

Moravia (as he claimed), who had previously deceived many potentates, was hanged because he defrauded Duke Frederick of Württemberg \*) of more than 2 tons of gold <note>In this context, a "ton of gold" was a fiscal unit representing 100,000 guilders rather than a measurement of weight.</note>, by pretending he could and would make gold from silver. The Duke had an elaborate gallows built, which weighed more than 25 hundredweight <term>Centner: a historical unit of weight, roughly 50 kilograms or 110 pounds</term>; he had the structure gilded and dressed the deceiver in a garment made entirely of tinsel gold <term>Lahngold: an imitation gold thread made of flattened copper or brass wire</term>, which also covered the gallows on which he \*\*) was hanged. A copperplate engraving of this event has been published.

1. That his name is Hans Heinrich von Müllenfels, and that previously, before His Imperial Majesty <note>original: *Kayl. Mayt.*, abbreviation for *Kaiserliche Majestät*</note> ennobled him, he was named Hans Heinrich Müller.

5. After completing his years of apprenticeship, he traveled to Hungary, and thereafter wandered to Breslau and Italy, specifically practicing his trade in Florence for half a year, and there he successfully treated <note>original: *feliciter curirt*</note> Adolf Wild, the Rhinegrave.

<meta>This page continues the confession of Hans Heinrich von Müllenfels. Having detailed his previous frauds against the Emperor and other princes, he now describes the specific "invention" he used to deceive the Duke of Württemberg at Kirchheim.</meta>

In the year 1604, the tests performed were all false and conducted with nothing but fraud. Since he was finally expected to deliver and perform his test as promised, but did not know by what means or way—because His Princely Grace <note>Referring to Duke Friedrich I of Württemberg</note> was watching him so closely—he could save himself and continue to deceive His Princely Grace, he devised an **invention** of a wonderful and strange cabinet with many drawers. He had such a cabinet made in Esslingen, lined on the inside with cloth and on the bottom with felt, so that if someone were inside, they would not be heard immediately. Finally, he had it brought to the Freihof <note>A "free farm" or manor exempt from certain taxes, located in Kirchheim unter Teck</note> in Kirchheim.

Inside this cabinet, three persons could stand and hide, and they could also look out through specially made holes at the top. He placed this cabinet against a wall in his **laboratory** <note>original: "laboratorio"</note>, and through the wall he made a hole with a sliding shutter <note>original: "Schelder," a dialect variant of a sliding panel or cover</note> that led into a small passage returning to a secret place <note>original: "rever. ad locum secretum"</note>. When the door was opened, the hole or small shutter was covered by the door so that it could not be noticed or seen. He had the wall and the shutter painted with the same color so that even less attention would be paid to it. 

All of this was done with the intent, will, and purpose that when he had to perform his test, someone could slip into the cabinet. Then, once the **lead** <note>symbol: $\hbar$ (Saturn)</note>, **mercury** <note>symbol: ☿ (Mercury)</note>, and other materials were publicly placed without fraud, and everyone had left the laboratory and it was locked with a **witness-seal** <term>Uhr:Kund: a formal sealing of a room, often involving a certificate or signature, to prove no tampering occurred</term>, that

However, while negotiations with the Pole were ongoing, Müllenfels managed to maneuver things so effectively that the estate of **Neidlingen** was granted to him, and the subjects there even swore their oaths of fealty to him. In return, he promised that whatever the Pole did not <term>perform</term> <note>original: "prästire," from the Latin "praestare"; to fulfill, provide, or demonstrate a skill</note>, he himself would perform. He then returned to Stuttgart and proposed to the Pole a means and a way by which he could get him out of the country safely and without incident. Thus, through strange and wonderfully devised means and a devilish proposal—as recorded in the official report—Müllenfels brought the Pole, who believed himself to be entirely rescued, out of Stuttgart, and

*) *Michael Sendivogius* (born 1556, died 1636) was in Germany four times. This event must have occurred during his third German journey. **Alexander Suchten**, or **Sethonius**, from Scotland, transformed 7 <term>Loth</term> <note>A historical unit of weight; 7 Loth is approximately 110 grams or 3.5 ounces.</note> of lead into gold (See Wegener’s *The Inept Adept* <note>original Latin: *Adeptus ineptus*</note>, p. 344 and following). In 1654, an alchemical society was founded in **Nuremberg**, which lasted until nearly the beginning of the 18th century. **Daniel Wülfer**, a preacher at St. Lawrence’s Church, was its director. The members consisted mostly of famous, learned, and wealthy people, for example, Dr. Jo...

While our gracious Prince and Lord showed every favor to the Polish Baron, and was always happy to tolerate, see, and have him in the land, Müllenfels falsely and deceitfully twisted His Princely Grace's words. He slandered the Princely reputation and highness in many ways with disparaging untruths, and, seduced by the evil spirit, committed such a shameful deed against the Pole. Thus, he asks once more for forgiveness. Especially on June 27th, when the Princely deputies had other matters to investigate, he once again confirmed everything—as contained herein and as he had previously confessed—steadfastly affirming that it was fraud and falsehood. With weeping eyes, he begged that someone might humbly <term>intercede</term> <note>original: "intercediren," meaning to formally plead for a mitigation of a sentence</note> on his behalf, so that he might be shown mercy in the final judgment: specifically, that his right hand and his head be struck off <note>In the 17th century, being beheaded was considered a "merciful" and more honorable death than being hanged or broken on the wheel.</note>.

->Done on June 28, the year 1606.<-

---

Hanns Heinrich Müllenfels, the imprisoned and accused, confesses under the <term>torture</term> <note>original Latin: "Tortur"</note> and the <term>repetition</term> <note>original Latin: "reiteration," referring to a second round of judicial interrogation</note> thereof undertaken against him according to law and judgment, as follows: That all his previously acknowledged <term>confession</term> <note>original: "Uhrgicht," a formal legal term for a statement or confession made during or after judicial torture</note>—upon the salvation and blessedness of his soul, as it was set down in writing from his own mouth and understood...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical riddles, comparing the previously mentioned Latin riddle for "Vitriol" with a Greek riddle for "Arsenic." It highlights Leibniz’s interest in these coded messages and his efforts to translate them.</meta>

But the **Basilian Riddle** <note>Referring to the legendary alchemist Basilius Valentinus</note> seems to differ from the Greek one *). So it is: yet there will perhaps be those who can devise a reconciliation. **"Our Vitriol, our Arsenic"** <term>Arsenic</term> <note>In alchemy, "Arsenic" often referred to the volatile, masculine principle of matter rather than just the poisonous element.</note> cry those who contend that they alone should be called philosophers. *) One of the oldest examples of such a riddle is found in the mystical verses of Philo of Tarsus, with which he describes his famous remedy against colic, which takes its name from him. He lived in the times of Augustus. Galen, *On the Composition of Medicines according to Localities*, Book IX, p. 297, Basel edition, 1538.

->The great discovery of the physician Philo of Tarsus for mortals,<-
->etc.<- <note>original Greek: "Ταρσεως ιατροιο μεγα θνητοισι Φιλωνος / Ευρεμα, etc."</note>

The alchemical riddle of **Stephanus** of Alexandria from the *Operations of the Great and Sacred Art* (addressed to Heraclius Augustus), in the sixth operation, is as follows:

I have nine letters, I am four-syllabled; consider me.
The first three syllables each have two letters,
The remaining syllable has the rest, and five are consonants.
The total sum of the number consists of twice seven hundreds,
And three times three tens, and twice three. If you know who I am,
You will not be uninitiated in the wisdom I possess.
<note>original Greek: "Εννεα γραμματ᾿ εχω, τετρασυλλαβος ειμι... Ουκ αμυητος εση της παρ᾽ εμου σοφιης." The total sum described is 1496.</note>

**Leibniz** translated them:

I am known by nine small letters, being myself four-syllabled:
A syllable has two, except that the last holds three.
There are four vowels, and five have no voice of their own <note>i.e., consonants</note>.
A century <note>the number 100</note> enters into the whole number twice seven times,
Then nine decades, then twice three. If you
Know me, then the approach to our sacred rites lies open.
<note>original Latin: "Litterulis noscor quadrisyllabus ipse novenis... hinc aditus ad sacra nostra patent."</note>

The braid-maker <note>German: "Bortenwürker"—a craftsman who makes ornamental borders, ribbons, or lace.</note> Heiling misled several people, who until then had earned an honest living, into his alchemical follies. Even in his old age, he misled a skilled turner and silversmith, Friedrich Kleinert, who from 1680 onward applied himself to the minting of medals. Kleinert particularly produced the edge inscriptions on these medals with great skill, which surpassed all others in their elegance *).

<note>This table demonstrates "isopsephy," a practice where the numerical values of letters in a word are summed to find a hidden meaning. Here, it decodes the Greek word for Arsenic (ΑΡΣΕΝΙΚΟΝ), transliterated as APCENIKON.</note>

The numbers from the row are arranged in columns. Under the second and third syllables, the numbers 200, 5, 50, and 1 are listed; next to them is the number 90 with the annotation "thrice three tens" <note>original Latin: "ter tres decades"</note> and the number 6 with the annotation "twice three" <note>original Latin: "bis tria"</note>. Under the fourth syllable (K O N) are 20, 70, and 50. At the far right of the calculation area, the total "1496" (the sum of 1400, 90, and 6) is written twice vertically.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the story of Friedrich Kleinert from the previous page. Having received a massive sum of money for alchemical secrets, he falls victim to fraudsters and financial ruin, culminating in a suicide attempt and a withdrawal from public life. It then shifts to academic citations regarding a famous alchemical encounter in 1666.</meta>

guilders promised for sharing his art, and of that amount, 30,000 fl. <note>fl. stands for florins or guilders, the local currency.</note> had already been paid out. Now Kleinert began to buy and build houses. Both his teacher, Christoph Heiling, who wanted to teach him the fixation of mercury <term>fixation of mercury: an alchemical process intended to turn liquid mercury into a solid, stable metal, usually seen as a step toward creating gold or silver</term>, and a vagabond named Julius Hartmann, a native of Freiberg who also promised to teach him how to make gold, deceived him shamefully. The latter ran off with 5,000 fl. that Kleinert had paid him. Kleinert could no longer pay the builders, he had to repay Meyer his money, and other creditors turned against him, so that he was placed in the town hall prison <term>Rathhausvogtey: the municipal jail located within the city hall complex</term>; on December 12, 1696, a strict mandate concerning alchemy <note>original German: "Alchymisterey"</note> was published. Kleinert was forced to resell the Ayrmann house in the Outer Laufer Lane <note>German: "äußern Laufergasse," a street in Nuremberg</note>, which he had bought for 9,000 fl., and his son-in-law <term>Eidam: an archaic German term for a son-in-law</term>, Georg Sebald Krauß, a deacon at St. Lawrence <note>original Latin abbreviation: "Diac. Laur.," referring to the Lorenzkirche in Nuremberg</note>, had to help pay the debts. Because of this, Kleinert became melancholic; on March 27, 1697, he jumped into the Pegnitz river from the Barefoot Bridge <note>German: "Barfüßer Brücke," a bridge in central Nuremberg</note> and floated on his back as far as the meat stalls <note>German: "Fleischbänke," the municipal meat market building</note>, where he was pulled out. He recovered, began to work again, no longer went out among people, and died on July 28, 1714, in his 82nd year.

Regarding the brass-smith <term>Rothgießer: literally "red-caster," a craftsman who works with copper alloys like brass or bronze</term> or Rosicrucian who visited Dr. Helvetius in The Hague on December 27, 1666, Wegener writes at length in *The Inept Adept* <note>original Latin: "Adepto inepto"</note>, p. 331 et seq., and Torbern Bergman in his *History of Chemistry*, p. 192 et seq., where the magnificent explanations of the late Wiegleb should be consulted.

<meta>This page transitions from the tragic downfall of the alchemist Friedrich Kleinert to a more scholarly investigation of a famous alchemical claim. Spinoza confirms this story of Dr. **Helvetius** (or Schweizer) in his 45th letter. 

I will first relate it in Spinoza’s own words, from *The Posthumous Works* <note>original Dutch title: *De nagelate Schriften* (Amsterdam, 1687, quarto), page 585</note>:

> Regarding the matter of **Helvetius**, I have spoken about it with Mr. **Vossius** <note>Isaac Vossius (1618–1689), a famous Dutch scholar and book collector</note>, who (not to recount in a letter everything we discussed) laughed at it greatly, and indeed showed himself surprised that I would ask him about such a trifle. However, disregarding this, I went to the **silversmith** named **Brechtelt**, who had tested the gold. But he, speaking quite differently than **Vossius**, said that the gold had increased during the melting and parting <term>parting: the chemical process of separating gold from silver</term>, and had become as much heavier as the weight of the silver he had added to the crucible <term>crucible: a ceramic or metal container in which metals may be melted at very high temperatures</term> for the separation; so that he firmly believed that this gold, which changed his silver into gold, had something extraordinary about it. Not only did he find it so, but also several other gentlemen who were present at the time. Afterwards, I went to **Helvetius** himself, who showed me the gold and the crucible, which was still gilded <gloss>covered in a thin layer of gold</gloss> on the inside, and told me that he had thrown no more than barely the size of a quarter of a grain of millet or mustard seed into the molten lead. He added that he would publish the account of the whole matter within a short time, and further said that in Amsterdam a man (and he believed this was the same one...

<summary>This page records the philosopher Baruch Spinoza's firsthand investigation into a famous alchemical transmutation claim made by Johann Friedrich Helvetius. ### Translation

I met with Mr. (**Isaac**) **Vossius** <note>Isaac Vossius (1618–1689), a renowned Dutch scholar and collector</note> regarding the **Helvetius** business, he writes to his friend J. J. (**Jarrig Jellis**), who (not to recount in this letter everything we discussed) laughed heartily; indeed, he was surprised that I would ask him about such trifles. However, considering this of little importance <note>original Latin: *flocci faciens*, literally "valuing it at a tuft of wool"</note>, I went to the goldsmith himself, whose surname is **Brechtelt**, who had tested the gold. He, however, held a far different view than Mr. **Vossius**, affirming that during the melting and parting <term>parting: the chemical process of separating gold from silver</term>, the weight of the gold had increased, and had been made heavier by exactly the weight of the silver he had cast into the crucible <term>crucible: a ceramic or metal container for melting metals at high temperatures</term> for the purpose of separation; so that he firmly believed that this gold, which had transmuted his silver into gold, contained something unique within itself. Nor was he alone; various other gentlemen, present at that time, also experienced this to be the case. After this, I went to **Helvetius** himself, who showed me both the gold and the crucible, the interior of which was even then still coated with gold; he told me that he had cast barely a quarter of a barleycorn or mustard seed into the molten lead. He added that he would soon publish the history of the whole business *), and further reported,

---
*) **Johann Friedrich Helvetius’s** *The Golden Calf, which the World Adores and Prays to* <note>original Latin: *Vitulus aureus, quem Mundus adorat et orat* (The Hague, 1667)</note>. In which is discussed the truest [miracle] of Nature...

<meta>This page concludes the letter from Baruch Spinoza to Jarrig Jellis regarding the alchemist Helvetius and transitions into a historical account by a later author concerning another alchemist, Krohnemann, citing 18th-century numismatic sources.</meta>

...a certain man (whom he thought was the very same one who had visited him) had performed the same operation <note>original Latin: "eandem operationem," referring to alchemical transmutation</note> in Amsterdam, about which you have no doubt heard. This is what I have been able to discover about this matter. — Voorburg, March 25, 1667. *Posthumous Works*, p. 533.

Mr. Cathedral Chaplain Spieß, in his *Brandenburg Historical Coin Amusements* <note>original: "Münzbelustigungen," a popular 18th-century genre of books detailing the history of coins and the stories behind them</note> (fourth year, p. 22), has shown from the official records what a fraudster **Krohnemann** <note>Christian Wilhelm von Krohnemann (1636–1686), a famous alchemical imposter who was eventually executed for his failed promises to create gold for the Margrave of Bayreuth</note> was. This is proven in particular by the report submitted by the then-Brandenburg-Culmbach mintmaster <term>mintmaster: the official in charge of a mint, responsible for the quality and weight of coinage</term>, titled:

### A Thorough, yet Truthful and Duty-Bound Report regarding the Amalgam <term>amalgam: a mixture of mercury with another metal, often used by alchemists as a base for transmutation</term> Prepared there by Krohnemann in the Smaller Vault of the Local Princely Castle.

Namely, the aforementioned **Krohnemann** [prepared] his so-called amalgam in the smaller vault of the said Princely Castle, in the high presence of His Serene Highness, my most gracious Prince and Lord, etc., as well as Her Serene Highness, my most gracious Prin—

---

> On the Miracle of Transmuting Metals, namely how the entire substance of lead was changed into pure gold <note>original Latin: "Aurum obryzum"</note> within a moment by any tiny particle of the true Philosopher’s Stone <note>original Latin: "Lapidis veri Philosophici"</note> at The Hague. Amsterdam, 1667. Octavo. Second edition, enlarged, same place, 1702. Octavo.

The elder **Dr. Johann Georg Volkamer** translated this book into German in Nuremberg in 1668. Another translation from the Dutch appeared in 1705 and 1726 in Frankfurt am Main. Following the second Latin edition, **Rothscholz** <note>Friedrich Roth-Scholtz (1687–1736), a prolific publisher and historian of alchemical and scientific texts</note> published it in German in 1727 in Nuremberg.

-cess \*) <note>completing "Princess" from the previous page</note> and ladies, etc., then Mr. Privy Councilor von Lilien \*\*). In two iron pans, he prepared liquid mercury <note>original Latin: *mercurium vivum*</note> with vinegar, verdigris <term>verdigris: a green pigment or crust formed on copper or brass, used in alchemical processes</term>, salt, and other ingredients, mixing it with a powder he brought in a white box. Subsequently, however, upon diligent investigation, it was found that these materials had been pre-mixed with pure gold. This will be more clearly revealed and sufficiently testified by those present at that time in the high presence of His Serene Highness and his Court Councilors, through the samples obtained by means of fire from the frequently mentioned amalgam <term>amalgam: a mixture of mercury with another metal</term>.

That the facts of the matter truly occurred as described above is testified by the undersigned with his own signature and imprinted seal <term>seal: an embossed or wax stamp used to authenticate a legal document</term>. Done at Bayreuth, March 10, 1686.

...[at] Kulmbach, he was hanged on the gallows \*) <note>completing the sentence from the previous page: "Krohnemann... was on April 27, 1686, hanged on the gallows at Kulmbach."</note>. He claimed to be able to "fix" mercury <note>original Latin: *mercurium fixum*; in alchemy, the "fixing" of mercury referred to the process of turning volatile liquid mercury into a solid, heat-resistant metal, a necessary step toward making gold</note>, and therefore the lines fastened above his head [at the gallows] ended thus:

->I was indeed intent on how mercury is fixed:<-
->Now the matter is reversed, and it is I who am fixed.<- <note>A dark pun on the alchemical term "fixing" versus being "fixed" or secured to the gallows.</note>

It is quite astonishing that such a crude fraud (I possess his portrait in miniature) could hold the highest positions of honor at the court of Bayreuth and, since the year 1677—at a time when Kunkel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous chemist and glassmaker who was skeptical of "gold-making" alchemists</note> was still living, whom one could have easily consulted—could deceive so many upright people.

One must be even more amazed that Caetano, twenty years later, greatly deceived the King of Prussia \*\*); just as at almost the same time

Johann Hektor von Klettenberg, the counterpart to Krohnemann, defrauded King Augustus of Poland <note>Augustus II the Strong</note> of a large sum. He was beheaded on February 29, 1720, at the Königstein Fortress. A. L. Schlözer's Correspondence, etc. Volume IX, Issue 50 <note>Roman numeral L stands for 50</note>, pages 88–100. This Klet:

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of correspondents of Dr. J. G. Volkamer the Elder, following the mention of his exchanges with Dr. Franz Gaßmann (Pantaleon).</meta>

**Johann Ludwig Hannemann** \*) in 1692, [corresponded] with **Kunckel** <note>Johann Kunckel (c. 1630–1703), a famous chemist and glassmaker</note> regarding **phosphorus** and **ruby glass** <note>a deep red glass made by adding gold chloride</note>, and with **Georg Ferdinand von Perney** (or **Pernauer**) from the year 1678 to 1685.

In the year 1678, a chemist who called himself **Salzacker** (or also **Rothmeyr**) was with him in Nuremberg; and in 1680, a certain **Damascenus** <note>likely an alchemical pseudonym</note> was there, who also performed much laboratory work with the apothecary **Johann Leonhart Stöberlein**. This **Damascenus** rightly said that one must proceed solely through **fermentation** and **precipitation**; he claimed that salts were the "keys to the bodies," but that they (as well as **vitriol** <note>sulfate minerals</note> and **antimony**) must first be freed from their sulfur and made **homogeneous** <note>consistent in composition</note> through both processes. He also stated that the true **spirit of wine** <note>highly distilled alcohol</note> must be released from its sulfur and prepared according to the teachings of **Basilius Valentinus** <note>a legendary, possibly mythical, 15th-century alchemist</note>. He required this for his **universal solvent** <note>original Latin: *solvente universali*; a hypothetical liquid capable of dissolving any substance</note>.

Among others, Volkamer wrote to **Gaßmann** as follows:

> In the *Alchemical Examination* <note>original Latin: *Examine Alchymistico*</note> (*Nuremberg* 1676, octavo), page 15, you made mention of a **heavy, milky liquid**.

---

[The text] appeared again in Gera in 1613, in octavo, under the title: **The Twin Mysteries of Antimony** <note>original Latin: *Antimonii mysteria gemina*</note>. In the year 1680, his chemical writings were published together in octavo at Frankfurt am Main. From the followers of this seeker comes the little rhyme: **Fix the sulfur of antimony with oil of vitriol** <note>original Latin: *Fige sulphur antimonii cum oleo Vitrioli*; "oil of vitriol" refers to concentrated sulfuric acid</note>.

\*) See his **Hermetic Egg** <note>original Latin: *Ovum hermeticum*</note> etc., Frankfurt 1694, octavo, page 384.

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical practitioners in Nuremberg, transitioning from the correspondence of Dr. Volkamer regarding heavy liquids and solvents to the story of Erasmus Schildkrot and the charitable foundations of the Landauer family.</meta>

I remember <note>original Latin: *Memini hicce Norimbergae...*</note> that here in Nuremberg there was a certain English doctor named **Schildkrot**, who, by the benefit of such a liquid, also extracted a **tincture** <note>a potent medicinal or alchemical extract</note>. But who besides an **Adept** <note>a master who has successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone</note> could interpret his philosophical description?

**Erasmus Schildkrot** was not an Englishman, but a Prussian, born in Königsberg, a famous physician of whom it was said in Nuremberg that he possessed the secret of making gold. It was not he, however, but **Marx Landauer** who, as early as 1467, on the **Thursday** <note>original German: *Pfinktag*</note> after St. Michael's Day (as the foundation charter states), established a foundation: that every week on each Friday, for two **hours** (*horae*) during the day, [charity be given to] twenty "house-poor" people <note>impoverished citizens who owned or rented a home but could not support themselves, distinguishing them from homeless beggars</note> who are male and female citizens of Nuremberg and do not beg publicly, nor receive the "rich alms" that the late **Burkart Sayler** (on the Saturday before St. Vitus Day, 1388) established for the maintenance of their physical nourishment. To each should be given one **shilling in gold**, of which twenty make one **guilder**, etc.

This **Marx Landauer** was a wealthy copper merchant and **coppersmith** <note>German: *Beckschlager*</note> in the Lower Coppersmiths' Lane. His son, **Matthäus Landauer** (*† 1515*), purchased the land for the **Twelve Brothers House** <note>a famous charitable home for retired craftsmen</note> behind All Saints on the Thursday after St. Othmar's Day, 1501. The foundation charter is dated on the Monday after Saint Sebastian's Day, the twenty-first day of the month of January, in the year of the birth of Christ our dear Lord, one thousand five hundred and ten. It was on January 23rd

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the charitable foundations in Nuremberg, specifically the Twelve Brothers House established by the Landauer family, and addresses the health regulations and legends surrounding its founders and their associates.</meta>

confirmed in this same year of 1510 by a Highly Noble Council. The **French disease** <note>a common historical term for syphilis, which spread rapidly through Europe in the late 15th century</note> must have already been very common back then, because a specific category for it appears:

### Falling sickness. Leprosy. and the French malady.
<note>original: "Hinfallend siechtagen. Aussatz. vnd mala Franzosa." "Falling sickness" was the historical term for epilepsy; "mala Franzosa" is Latin for the French sickness.</note>

If, however, one of the brothers should be afflicted with **leprosy**, the **falling sickness**, or the **French disease**, he shall not be tolerated among the other brothers, but instead be dismissed immediately. I consider the entire tale of **Dr. Schildkrot's** friendship with **Matthes Landauer** to be a fable, much like the description of the **Gold-Flower** on the **Mögeldorf meadows** <note>Mögeldorf is a district of Nuremberg; the "Gold-Flower" refers to an alchemical legend of a plant that could transition base metals into gold</note> near Nuremberg, which was printed in 1633 and is repeated in **Peter Johann Fabri’s** *Brightly Shining Sun on the Alchemical Firmament*, etc. (Nuremberg, 1705, octavo), No. 7. **J. A. Böner** engraved **Matthes Landauer’s** portrait in copper in 1666.

---

*) Too little caution was used: one could also (though admittedly very rarely) be infected in an innocent way, at least with a type of leprosy. Thus, in 1506, **Albrecht Dürer** <note>the famous German Renaissance artist</note> wrote from Venice on August 18th to **Pirckheimer**: — “Tell our Prior (at the Augustinians) to pray to God for me, that I may be protected, and especially from the **Frenchmen** <note>original: "vor den Franzosen," a contemporary euphemism for syphilis</note>, for I know of nothing that I fear more now, as nearly everyone has it. Many people are completely consumed by it, so that they die.” See my *Journal of Art and Literature*, Vol. X, p. 24.

<meta>This page continues a technical discussion regarding alchemical substances and processes, following the account of Wenzel Seiler's experiments. It focuses on the properties of antimony and critiques various alchemical authorities.</meta>

<margin>*) See <note>Johann Heinrich Gottlob von Justi (1717–1771) was a famous German mineralogist and political economist.</note> von Justi’s chemical writings, Part I, page 42 of the second edition. This is the well-known <term>Particular</term> <note>A "Particular" process in alchemy refers to a method intended to transmute a specific amount of base metal into gold or silver, rather than the "Universal" method of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> of David Beuther, 1582, according to which the <term>regulus of antimony</term> <note>original: "Spiesglanzkönig"; the metallic form of antimony separated from its ore.</note> must be melted with iron filings and chalk for so long until it becomes porous, as if it had been sucked dry by bees. However, Beuther’s entire printed instruction is nothing but hot air. Schwärzer’s works are much better.</margin>

That the <term>regulus of antimony</term>, a semi-metal which has absolutely no power over gold, could never serve as the primary material for the refinement of metals through the "Universal Way" <note>The "Universal Way" refers to the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone, capable of perfecting all matter.</note>, is known to all great chemists; they laugh at Basilius Valentinus’s so-called <term>Triumphal Chariot of Antimony</term> <note>original: "Triumphwagen des Antimonii"; a famous alchemical text published in the early 17th century attributed to a legendary monk.</note> if one expects to learn the art of gold-making from it. One need only read what the great Kunckel <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703) was a celebrated chemist and glassmaker who was skeptical of many alchemical claims.</note> writes in his *Chemical Laboratory*, page 452. In short, because the regulus of antimony contains too little acidic sulfuric salt, it is impossible for it to achieve anything significant. Advanced chemists will understand me. The false and mistakenly named <term>gold-calx</term> <note>A powder (oxide) produced by heating a metal; alchemists often believed these residues were steps toward transmutation.</note> obtained through the regulus of antimony made with iron (the <term>martial regulus</term> <note>original: "regulus martialis"; "Mars" was the alchemical symbol for iron.</note>) vanishes into smoke and slag on the <term>cupel</term> <note>A shallow, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals.</note>. Because this residue is much blacker than true gold-calx, alchemists call it <term>blacker than black</term> <note>original: "nigrius nigro"</note> and attempt to use it for the "Particular Way" to refine silver into gold *). Hungarian antimony often carries gold within it, and Mr. von Justi once obtained a gold separation from antimony in a process that yielded nearly three ducats' worth for every mark of silver. ### From a letter by Johann Böhm, from Venice on January 22, 1683, to Dr. Johann Georg Volkamer, the father.

> — I know of nothing new except for this, which was communicated to me.

Recipe: Take 1 part common sulfur; let it melt in a crucible like oil. Throw 2 parts of <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurii"; quicksilver.</note> into it; stir the mercury constantly into the sulfur with an iron spatula until they finally mix, and a black mass is formed from both. Amalgamate this with a strong <term>oil of sulfur</term> <note>original: "oleo Sulphuris"; likely a concentrated sulfuric acid or a sulfurous solution used as a solvent.</note> until it becomes a thin mass. Wrap 1 or 2 loth of this in a small cloth; wrap even more cloths around it so that the mercury comes to rest right in the center; tie it tightly together with twine. Place this bundle into a strong charcoal fire; leave it there until it is glowing red all the way through; take it out, let it cool, and remove the coals and cloths. If it is done correctly, you will find the mercury burned into a red powder in the center. It is completely <term>fixed</term> <note>In alchemy, a substance is "fixed" if it remains stable and does not vaporize when exposed to high heat.</note>, but has no <term>ingress</term> <note>original: "ingress"; the ability of an alchemical substance to penetrate and transform the internal nature of a base metal.</note>. However, if one can grant it such power, it is a certain <term>tincture</term> <note>In this context, a "tincture" is a medicinal or transmuting agent capable of "coloring" or changing the properties of matter.</note> *).

---

The sentence is incomplete at the end of the page.</note>

A certain **Dr. Johann Konrad Bardili**, a city physician in Nürtingen, in the region of Württemberg, wrote extensively to Dr. Volkamer between 1668 and 1669 regarding chemical processes. I have seen this in his letters, which are now kept in the Trew Library <note>The Trewische Bibliothek was the significant scientific library of the physician Christoph Jacob Trew (1695–1769).</note> at Altdorf; however, these are common matters, which are better understood in our day than they were back then.

## 18th Century.

### Certified Copy of the Document Concerning the Completed Transmutation of Metals.

->Vienna, Austria, July 19, 1716.<-

Recorded in Vienna on July 19, 1716, being the 7th Sunday after Trinity, at the lodgings of Mr. Wolff Philipp Panzer, Court Councilor to the Prince of Schwarzburg, located on the Carinthian Bastion. This was held within the house of His Excellency Count Carl Ernst von Rappach, Imperial General Field Marshal as well as Colonel Master of Provincial and Household Ordnance and Commandant of the Imperial Residence and Principal Fortress of Vienna, in the presence of His Excellency Count Joseph von Würben and Freudenthal, Imperial Privy Councilor and Bohemian Vice-Chancellor of the German Expedition, etc.

Metallic matter always contains mercury within it as a base, and secondly, it contains a most subtle, ignited sulfur as a coagulant, which is highly probable. Take only mercury and hold it over the fumes of lead; it will be fixed <note>original: "fixabitur"; in alchemy, "fixing" refers to the process of turning a volatile liquid substance, like mercury, into a solid that can withstand the heat of a fire without evaporating.</note> immediately; therefore, only the smallest amount of fixing matter is needed to fix mercury. If such an acid can now be found, gold will easily be prepared.

...of Mr. Ernst, Royal Prussian actual Privy State Councilor and currently Minister residing at the Imperial Court, etc., and Mr. Wolff, Privy Councilor to the Princely Houses of Brandenburg-Kulmbach and Ansbach and Envoy to the Imperial Diet—the brothers being respectively Count and Baron von Metternich—as well as the aforementioned Court Councilor of Schwarzburg and his son, Johann Christoph Philipp Panker.

1. At 10 o'clock in the forenoon, the aforementioned persons gathered at the previously designated place. 2. Those present weighed two copper pennies. 3. The first [penny] was allowed to become red-hot upon a charcoal fire. The Schwarzburg Court Councilor took it out of the embers with a small pair of tongs, whereupon Mr. Wolff, Baron von Metternich, picked up the aforementioned white grain with a small stick of wax—as it could not be grasped otherwise—and with it...

<meta>This page continues the eyewitness account of an alchemical transmutation performed in Vienna in July 1716. The previous page described the weighing of a tiny white grain and several copper coins in the presence of high-ranking officials.</meta>

...as quickly as possible, [one] moved [the grain] around across the surface <note>original: *in superficie*</note> on only one side of the aforementioned glowing copper penny.

4. The Bohemian Vice-Chancellor, who was concerned that the penny might become fluid <note>meaning he feared the coin would melt entirely and lose its shape</note>, threw the penny into the water—even though the white grain was still lying together on top of it and the penny still appeared red-hot—and snatched it out again so quickly that he burned his fingers; whereupon

5. Everyone saw with their own eyes that the red penny, having been thrown into the water, was pulled out white again, with certain signs that it had already truly begun to melt.

6. Because it was observed that the grain had also gone into the water, they also made the aforementioned <term>Polturac: a Hungarian copper coin of low value</term> glowing hot and simply threw it into the water; it was likewise immediately pulled out white again, and this coin was given to Baron Wolff von Metternich.

7. They did not stop there, but took two other smaller copper pennies—the kind distributed here at the Poor House—made them glowing hot together and threw them both into the aforementioned water. Upon being pulled out, it was found that they had changed color significantly, but had not become entirely white. The aforementioned Metternich brothers took these two coins for themselves.

8. A square piece of **copper sheet** was also thrown, red-hot, into this water; it was found that it changed color in several places, though less so than the two previous copper pennies.

9. From this aforementioned copper sheet, a small, narrow sliver was cut. This shaving was heated to a glow for a second time and once again thrown into the water, from which it emerged completely white.

10. They tried this again with another such sliver from the same copper sheet, but found that it came out unchanged.

11. The larger penny mentioned in number 2 was cut in half, and it was found to be white all the way through. One half was taken by the aforementioned Count Ernst von Metternich, and the other by Baron Wolff von Metternich.

12. From the half taken by the latter, a small piece—at the aforementioned weight of 2 pounds <note>original: ℔; while the symbol usually means pounds, in assaying it often refers to a proportional "assay pound" used to determine purity ratios</note>—was placed upon the <term>cupel: a shallow, porous ceramic dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals through high heat</term>. Upon calculation, it was found that this copper penny had been transformed into 14-loth silver <note>The "loth" was a unit of fineness; 16 loth represented pure silver, so 14-loth silver would be approximately 87.5% pure</note>.

13. The small sliver mentioned in number 9 was also placed upon the cupel, and it was found that it had been transformed into 12-loth silver.

15. Since it could no longer be doubted that the copper had become good, true silver, the weight was also investigated. For this purpose, the two pennies designated as No. 2, now transformed into silver, were weighed a second time. The first weighed 125 pounds 8 loth, thus 25 pounds more [than its original weight], while the other weighed 79 pounds 16 loth, thus 11 pounds more <note>The symbol ℔ refers to the "assay pound," a proportional unit. 16. It was not possible to calculate with total precision how many parts of copper one part of this <term>tincture: the concentrated alchemical substance, often a powder, used to "tinge" or transform base metals</term> had turned into silver, because the small pennies previously designated as No. 7 and the copper sheet mentioned in No. 8 had not yet been <term>parted: the chemical process of separating precious metals from base metals to determine purity</term>. Therefore, one may say without much fear of error that one part of this [tincture] has transformed [many] thousand parts.

Done at the place and on the day as stated above <note>original Latin: "Actum Loco et die ut supra"</note>, in memory and as a testimony to the event thus carried out, and the true transmutation performed by us, the undersigned <term>eyewitnesses: the German "Ocularen Zeugen" emphasizes that these men saw the event with their own eyes</term>,

...caused a stir, namely that from one <term>Mark of silver <note>a weight of approximately 233 grams</note></term>, if four <term>Loth <note>a unit of weight, roughly 14.6 grams</note></term> of gold were added, after deducting this gold and all costs required for materials, a surplus of six <term>ducats <note>high-purity gold coins</note></term> should result, which actually occurred. This process confirms **Homberg’s** <note>Wilhelm Homberg, a famous 17th-century chemist</note> experiment and proves clearly that particles are present in silver that have an inclination to become gold, and that metals can be ennobled.

The great and singular King **Frederick** <note>Frederick II, "the Great," of Prussia</note>, a few years before the Seefeld affair <note>referring to the alchemist Johann Christoph von Seefeld</note>, was persuaded by **Fredersdorf** <note>Michael Gabriel Fredersdorf, the King’s chamberlain and confidant</note>—through a **Frau von Pfuel** from Saxony, who came to Potsdam with two very beautiful daughters who practiced the alchemical trade skillfully—to spend over ten thousand <term>thalers <note>large silver coins</note></term> on such attempts. Finally, they made small red grains out of gold, which looked almost like rubies and seemed to have nothing metal-like about them. But, to transform fifty ducats into such red grains, one loses about six ducats. **Frederick** thus laughed at alchemy based on experience *).

<meta>This page continues the author's account of his time in London (1757–1762), moving from the general mania for alchemy to a specific case study of a Saxon clergyman, Christoph Leberecht Chrysel, who fell victim to the era's obsession with mineralogy and chemical secrets.</meta>

*Alchemy is a rod of correction for weak people and fools, so that wise men leave it well alone.*

It is easy to believe that neither the Doctor nor the goldsmith <note>Referring to Dr. Scheidet and the unnamed goldsmith mentioned on the previous page</note> found any edification in this; at the very least, they grew no wiser, and the time that followed punished their folly terribly.

In the year 1761, I read in the **Gentleman's Magazine** for August, p. 369, the moving complaints of a cheated German chemist. Shortly thereafter, I became personally acquainted with the man himself at the home of the distiller **Erfurt** from **Nuremberg**, who lived in **Chelsea**. This distiller had invented a crucible compound <note>A material used to make melting pots for metals</note> that withstood testing as well as the famous Hessian ones <note>Hessian crucibles were prized across Europe for their exceptional durability under intense heat</note>. The man was a clergyman from Saxony named **Christoph Leberecht Chrysel**. He had five children to feed and lived primarily by assaying <note>term: assaying: the chemical process of testing ore and minerals to determine their ingredients and quality</note> minerals and ore samples. **Lord Egremont** <note>Charles Wyndham, 2nd Earl of Egremont, a prominent British statesman</note> frequently made use of his skill. I advised him to complain in detail of the injustice done to him to the entire London public. This took place in a publication of 22 large octavo pages, which bore the title:

> The dreadful Case of an unfortunate stranger, **C. L. C.** mentioned in the **Gentleman's Magazine** for August 1761 and Pages 369 and 370 thereof given a too short and insufficient Abridgment; Represented now in a more extensive Account — by himself the unfortunate and wrongfully oppressed sufferer **Christopher Lebrecht Chrysel**. The 10th of November, 1761.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Christoph Leberecht Chrysel, the Saxon clergyman and chemist introduced on the previous page. Having established Chrysel’s credentials and his struggle in London, the author now details the specific deception that led to his ruin.</meta>

The sad story is as follows:

<margin>*) He possessed a fortune of at least 10,000 pounds sterling and had two children.</margin>
Since his arrival in London in 1757, Mr. Chrysel had lived in middle Moorfield and placed advertisements in the newspapers stating that he assayed minerals and ore samples at a reasonable price. This attracted many acquaintances among both high- and low-born <term>lovers of chemistry</term> <note>Amateur scientists and hobbyists who were fascinated by chemical experiments and metallurgy during the Enlightenment</note>. Among others, an English goldsmith who lived near St. Paul’s Cathedral*) sought to gain his friendship and trust. For two months, he spoke of nothing but religion, piety, and honesty; eventually, he warned Chrysel about certain speculators, known in London as <term>Schemers</term> <note>A contemporary term for project-makers or con artists who proposed fraudulent or impossible scientific schemes</note>, and frequently invited him to his home; however, Chrysel did not wish to come. At last, he one day showed him an alchemical recipe for a process which he had received from a learned Lord, and requested that he build him an <term>athanor</term>, or a <term>lazy Heinz</term> <note>original: "Athenor, oder faulen Heinzen"; a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant temperature for long periods, essential for alchemical work</note>. Chrysel warned him against these <term>alchemical whims</term> <note>original: "Goldmachersgrillen" — literally "gold-maker's crickets," a phrase used to describe the obsessive or eccentric delusions of those seeking to create gold</note>. The goldsmith did not cease in his invitations; finally, Chrysel visited him, and there the fool paraded his wealth and his <term>pietism</term>, his almsgiving and his shows of devotion before him. He showed him six fine copper engravings in his dining room with the mottos: "I was hungry," etc., "I was thirsty," etc. <note>original: "Ich war hungrig 2c. Ich war durstig 2c." — These are references to the biblical Works of Mercy from Matthew 25:35</note>, and did not stop pleading for him to build an <term>athanor</term> in order to carry out the process. Chrysel wanted nothing to do with the "precious" process and left the fellow to muddle through it alone.

The arrest for debt took place at the <term>Wood Street Compter</term> <note>The Wood Street Compter was a notorious debtor's prison in the City of London, known for its harsh conditions.</note>. The <term>bailiff</term> <note>original: "Bailiff, oder Gefangennehmer"; a legal officer authorized to make arrests for debt.</note>, or officer of the arrest, returned and said that the goldsmith was bedridden and wished to grant Mr. Chrysel a full release, provided that all his papers, and the guarantees regarding the laboratory and the <term>indigo factory</term> <note>As noted previously, indigo was a highly valuable blue dye, making this factory a significant industrial asset.</note>, were returned to him. Chrysel expressed his astonishment that the royal debt-prison official had not gone to the judge who had ordered his imprisonment to request a hearing. 

But the good man was told that this could not happen for at least two months, and if he did not have money and good lawyers, he could remain in prison for many years without a judge taking any notice of him. It is easy to imagine how much Chrysel was terrified by these "comforting" words <note>The author uses "tröstlichen" (comforting) ironically here, as the news was actually devastating.</note>. If he wished to be released, he now had to comply—as a man without money or assistance—and sign whatever they demanded of him. The goldsmith did not even allow him fresh laundry for eight weeks. Even three months later, he still addressed him with contemptuous words. 

After an arrest of forty days, he was set at liberty once again on the Saturday before Easter. On April 7th, he went to the country house with two friends to look after the belongings he had left behind and to fetch clean laundry. At first, the servants did not want to let him in. He found his room open, and everything cleared away except for his desk. Even this had been broken into; his papers and correspondence had been stolen. He sent word four times in London to the gold- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "Gold-," referring to the goldsmith.</note>

In the meantime, publicizing his grievances to the London public served to ensure that the honest Chrysel was greatly pitied, and he earned much money, as before, by <term>assaying</term> <note>original: "Probirung"; the process of testing metals and minerals for purity and composition.</note> minerals and ore specimens. ## Platinum and Mercury.

<note>original: *Platina und Quecksilber*. In the 18th century, platinum was often referred to by its Spanish name, "platina" (little silver). Mercury was commonly called "quicksilver" in scientific and alchemical texts of this period.</note>

<meta>Page 135 continues a survey of contemporary 18th-century chemical literature and the transition from older alchemical traditions to modern experimental chemistry.</meta>

Most minerals and natural products have now been chemically examined in the most precise manner, such as the excellent treatises on arsenic and mercury by Messrs. *Torbern Bergman* <note>Torbern Bergman (1735–1784) was a Swedish chemist and mineralogist famous for developing the first tables of chemical affinity.</note> and *Georg Friedrich Hildebrandt*. In his writing on the production of saltpeter <note>Saltpeter, or potassium nitrate, was a vital resource for making gunpowder and was a major focus of chemical research at the time.</note>, *J. A. Weber* tests the theories of the best chemists of former times through experiments he performed. He found them all to be false, rejecting the old favorite opinions of the followers of *Glauber* <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670) was an influential alchemist and chemist who discovered sodium sulfate, still called "Glauber’s salt."</note> and *Stahl* <note>Georg Ernst Stahl (1659–1734) was the founder of the "phlogiston theory," which argued that a fire-like element called phlogiston was contained within combustible bodies.</note>, and asserts that the entire business of saltpeter production rests solely on the air.

---

We now recognize these as metal oxides.</term> in every metal is again different, so that it may not yet have been possible for anyone to truly transform the earth of one metal into the earth of another; yet the difference, as Professor *Johann Friedrich Gmelin* *) says, appears rather [to be based] on

Platinum is regarded as a third of the noble and perfect metals, with the charac—

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of platinum from page 136, completing the description of its appearance and transitioning into its historical discovery and early scientific literature.</meta>

...fine-grained silver from the mines of Pinto and Santa Fe, belongs among the perfect metals. It shares many characteristics with gold and is as significant a phenomenon for the mineral kingdom as electricity is for experimental physics.

This metal, which has been known in Europe for 63 years, is most frequently found in Peru in the region of Cartagena <note>modern-day Colombia; at the time, this was part of the Spanish Viceroyalty of Peru</note>. Charles Wood, an English metallurgist, was the first to bring a sample of it from Jamaica to England in 1741. He \*), along with Scheffer \*\*) and Lewis \*\*\*), were the first to write about it and publish the results of the experiments they conducted with it. Antonio de Ulloa was the first among the Spaniards to mention it in his 1748 travelogue to Peru under the name *Platina* <note>Spanish for "little silver"</note>. In the year 1758, a book was published in Paris titled: *Platinum, white gold, or the eighth metal* <note>original French: "La Platine, l'or blanc, ou le huitieme metal"</note>. It contains all previously [published works where it was] designated [under the charac]ter of the comet <note>Early chemists often assigned astronomical symbols to metals; platinum was briefly associated with the symbol for a comet before receiving its own chemical shorthand</note>. According to the new chemical nomenclature, *Platina* is called *Platin* or *Platinum*.

\*) *Philosophical Transactions* <note>original Latin: "Philos. Transact."</note> 1749 and 1750.

\*\*) *Swedish Treatises* <note>original German: "Schwedische Abhandlungen"</note>, 1751.

\*\*\*) *Philosophical Transactions* 1754. Volume 50, Part 1, numbers 19 and 20.

In the year 1753, the Spanish Ministry handed a small bag of platinum to Mr. William Bowles for examination. See Bowles' *Introduction to the natural history and physical geography of Spain; translated by the Viscount of Flavigny. Paris, 1776. Large octavo. Pages 174–195* <note>original French title: "Introduction à l'hist. naturelle et à la Géographie phisique de l'Espagne"</note>.

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the scientific literature on platinum, specifically addressing theories regarding its composition as a natural alloy of gold and iron, referencing the works of Count Buffon, Marggraf, and others.</meta>

...published writings about this strange metal.

The Count of Buffon *) rightly considers **platinum** not to be a residue from the first imperfect Spanish processing of American gold ores using mercury, sulfur, and iron **); but rather a mixture of gold and iron sand originating from nature itself, since it is roughly 1/12th lighter than gold, and perhaps only 1/5th of its weight is iron. He melted one-quarter iron with three-quarters gold and obtained a mass that was equal to platinum in color and weight. The more finely the platinum is crushed, the more the magnet acts upon it; conversely, spheres of melted platinum are insensitive to the magnet. Also, the fact that Prussian blue <note>a synthetic blue pigment, the first of its kind; its production was used here as a chemical test to detect the presence of iron</note> is produced through the platinum proves the presence of iron.

I remember having read a treatise by Mr. Marggraf <note>Andreas Sigismund Marggraf (1709–1782), a pivotal German chemist and pioneer of analytical chemistry</note> about platinum in a volume of the *Mineralogical Amusements* <note>original German: "mineralogischen Belustigungen," a popular 18th-century scientific periodical</note>. He had far too little of it to be able to conduct decisive experiments with it. It is very difficult to obtain unadulterated platinum in Europe...

---

**) Count von Milly held this highly incorrect opinion; he [produced] a true Prussian blue <note>original German: "Berlinerblau"</note> from the...

-fect amalgam, which all at once showed fusibility under the great Trudaine burning-glass <note>The Trudaine burning-glass was a massive lens constructed by Jean-Charles-Philibert Trudaine de Montigny for the French Academy of Sciences to achieve extreme temperatures.</note> *). The Count obtained pure platinum by either precipitating the solution of raw platinum with blood-lye <term>blood-lye: potassium ferrocyanide, a chemical used at the time to identify and remove iron from solutions</term> until no more blue sediment fell, then separating this, and placing the red salt—which settled partly on its own and partly after evaporation—into the fire, where, like all metallic salts of noble metals, it restored itself to its metallic state on its own. Alternatively, he allowed the platinum to deflagrate <note>To burn or oxidize rapidly</note> with saltpeter, leached it afterwards, extracted the mixed-iron particles with vitriolic acid <term>vitriolic acid: sulfuric acid</term>, and dissolved the remaining calx <term>calx: the powdery metallic residue or oxide left after heating</term>, after he had washed it, in aqua regia <term>aqua regia: "royal water," a powerful mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids</term>, precipitating it with blood-lye, and melting it with a simple glass flux; however, by this latter method, he obtained only a very brittle regulus <term>regulus: the pure metallic mass that forms at the bottom of a crucible during smelting</term>. <meta>This page continues the physical description of purified platinum, detailing its density and malleability compared to other metals. It also references the work of the Swedish chemist Torbern Bergman, who conducted experiments to prove that platinum, nickel, cobalt, and manganese are distinct metallic elements rather than alloys or variations of iron.</meta>

[is brought to a] white heat, hammered, forged, and drawn into wire; it can even be amalgamated <note>original: "verquicken" — the process of combining a metal with mercury</note> with boiling mercury just like silver, when introduced into it. Now it is no longer attracted by a magnet and possesses a bright, lasting luster more similar to silver. It has a specific gravity <term>specific gravity: a measure of density relative to water; here used to compare the weight of equal volumes of material</term> that is greater than that of gold—bearing a ratio of 27 to 25—and a toughness that, while less than that of copper, brass, and iron, is greater than that of silver and gold.

The Knight Bergmann <note>Torbern Olof Bergman (1735–1784), a pioneering Swedish chemist and mineralogist</note> *) has conducted precipitation experiments with platinum, nickel, cobalt, and magnesium <note>In the 18th century, "magnesium" usually referred to manganese; the element we call magnesium today was not isolated in its metallic form until 1808</note>, four metals that were first properly tested in Sweden. These experiments serve to further demonstrate that these natural products truly deserve the name of distinct metals. For example, platinum cannot be composed of gold and iron, as it is not decomposed or broken down by zinc. The other three metals are likewise precipitated by zinc only due to foreign admixtures; one must not regard them as modified forms of iron until their production from pure iron can actually be achieved.

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general physical properties of platinum to specific metallurgical techniques for shaping and working the metal. It describes the pioneering chemical methods used by European scientists like Achard and Scheffer to overcome platinum's high melting point through the use of arsenic.</meta>

Director Achard <note>Franz Karl Achard (1753–1821), a Prussian chemist famous for developing the process to produce sugar from beets, but also a pioneer in platinum metallurgy</note> *) in Berlin has provided a method for obtaining vessels made of platinum by melting it with arsenic. Specifically, one melts this metal with equal parts arsenic and salt of tartar <note>original: "Weinsteinsalz" — potassium carbonate, often used as a flux in chemistry</note>; then, one powders the brittle arsenical platinum and fills the gaps of a double, interlocking mold with it, and places it in a fierce fire. The arsenic evaporates <note>original: "verfliegt" — literally "flies away"; arsenic sublimes, turning directly from a solid to a gas when heated</note>, and the platinum takes the shape of the mold, requiring only a bit of hammering over a mandrel <note>original: "Dorn" — a shaped tool or rod used as a core for forging metal into hollow shapes</note> to finish it.

Mr. Scheffer **) found that arsenic brings platinum into flux <note>meaning it lowers the melting point enough to make it liquid and workable</note> quickly. Lewis and the Count of Sickingen found this property very imperfect in crystalline arsenic. Achard mixed potash <note>original: "Potasche"</note> into it and succeeded very well in achieving the melting of the platinum. Two drams <term>Quentchen: an old German unit of weight, approximately 3.6 to 4 grams</term> of this metal yielded a good, smooth grain that weighed just as much while still mixed with arsenic. When all the arsenic had been driven off under the muffle <term>muffle furnace: a furnace in which the subject material is isolated from the fuel and ashes by a ceramic chamber</term>, the platinum was solid, silver-colored, and ductile like gold, though with a loss of 56 grains <term>Gran: a small unit of weight, roughly 0.06 grams</term>.

---

*) *Chemical Annals* <note>original: "Chemische Annalen"</note> etc. by Lorenz Crell. Volume I, Helmstädt, 1784. No. I.

**) *Almanac or Pocketbook for Assayers* <note>original: "Almanach oder Taschenbuch für Scheidekünstler" — a "Scheidekünstler" (literally "separation artist") was an early term for a chemist or refiner</note>, 1786.

It had been heavily compressed by a rolling mill <term>laminoir: a machine with heavy rollers used to flatten metal into sheets</term>, weighed 80 grains <term>Gran: an old unit of weight, approximately 0.06 grams</term>, and lost 3 5/8 in water; thus its specific gravity <note>original: "eigne Schwere"; the density of a substance relative to water</note> was 22,069; that which had passed through a wire-drawing die <term>filiere: a metal plate with holes used to pull metal into thin wire</term> was only 21,041; raw cast platinum was 14,626, and purified was 19,530. <note>These figures represent density where water is 1,000; in modern terms, 22.069, etc. Modern pure platinum is approximately 21.45.</note>

In Rome, an excellent chemist made platinum malleable, so that he hoped to manufacture kitchen utensils. In November 1788, the late King of Spain presented a chalice, paten, and spoon (as is customary to give during the Mass in Spain) made of this metal to the Pope. The royal goldsmith Alon manufactured two cruets <note>original: "Opferkännchen" — small vessels used to hold the wine and water for the Eucharist</note> for the court chapel in 1790.

However, at a specific temperature in oxygen gas <note>original: "säurezeugenden Gas"</note>, it can be brought to a glowing state without actually melting; in this state, it indeed absorbs the oxygen from the gas and becomes oxidized. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Sigismund Friedrich Hermbstädt's observations on the oxidation of platinum, concluding a sentence from the previous page regarding the behavior of oxygen at varying temperatures.</meta>

substance is then released again, its attraction to caloric <term>original: "Wärmestoff," a hypothetical fluid then believed to represent the physical substance of heat</term> increases once more, and it escapes as oxygen gas <note>original: "säurezeugendes Gas" — literally "acid-generating gas," the term used by proponents of Lavoisier’s chemistry</note>. Thus, while the metal oxidizes at one moment, it is reduced <note>returned to its metallic state from an oxide</note> again in the next due to the increasing heat; as a result, the concept of a **noble metal** is nothing more than the specific temperature state at which this metal can remain combined with the oxygen <note>original: "säurezeugenden Stoffe"</note>.

## Mercury.

Mercury <note>Greek: "Hydrargyros" (Υδραργυρος), meaning "water-silver"; Latin: "Argentum vivum," meaning "living silver"</note>—discussed by Dioscorides in Book V, chapter 64; by Pliny in Book XXIII, and under "Living Silver" in chapters 20, 32, and 41; and by Galen in *On the Powers of Simple Medicines*, Book IX—was considered by Basil Valentine <note>a legendary 15th-century alchemist known for his work on antimony</note> and Paracelsus <note>the famous Swiss-German physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals in medicine</note> to be one of their primary elements. Reference can be found in the *Triumphal Chariot of Antimony*, page 325. Paracelsus writes in *On the Secrets of Creation*: "And God the Almighty—called." In his work *Opus Paramirum*, he states: "There are three substances that give each thing its body: Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt." <note>original German: "Drey sind der Substanz, die da einem jeglichem sein corpus geben, Sulphur, Mercurius, Sal." This refers to the *Tria Prima*, the three fundamental principles of Paracelsian alchemy.</note>

Johann Joachim Becher <note>a 17th-century German physician and scholar who helped develop the theory that eventually became Phlogiston theory</note> assumed a "material earth" <term>original: "Materialerde"; Becher categorized types of 'earths' as the basic building blocks of matter</term> as the fundamental substance of bodies. Paracelsus called mercury the "water of the metals."

It is the true deceiver and impostor of the alchemists <note>original Latin: "illusor und impostor chymicorum," referring to how mercury vanishes into vapor when heated, seemingly "cheating" the chemist</note>. *Neumann, chemical lectures, page 1603.*

The mercury ore consisting of sulfur and mercury is called cinnabar <note>Greek: "Kinnabaris" (Κινναβαρις)</note>. *)

---

*) *Dioscorides, Book V, chapter 63. Galen, On the Powers of Simple Medicines, Book IX.* In that miserable book,

Then wash it <note>original: *abgesüsset*, literally 'sweetened'; the process of washing a substance with water to remove acidic salts</note> as much as possible, and finally draw a rectified spirit <note>original: *Branntwein*; distilled alcohol used here as a solvent or to further purify the substance</note> from it, for the seventh or ninth time, or until it glows in the fire and does not smoke away: then you have **Diaphoretic precipitated mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium praecipitatum diaphoreticum*; a preparation intended to induce sweating for medicinal purposes</note>.”

This instruction, as Mr. **Hildebrandt** (*History of Mercury*, p. 154) notes, agrees with that of the **Prussian-Brandenburg Dispensatory** <note>original: *Dispensatorii Borusso-Brandenburgici*; an official pharmacopoeia or book of medicinal standards</note>, page 83.
