Mercury, when it is frozen in a high degree of cold \*), can be hammered, according to the observations of Braun \*\*) and Hutchins \*\*\*); according to Braun, it is more flexible than gold and lead, but when bent sharply, it is more brittle than tin and has a dull sound. According to Hutchins, it sounds like lead.

The fluidity of mercury is only a higher degree of fusibility <note>original: *Schmelzbarkeit*; the property of being able to be melted</note>, which it possesses in the highest degree among all metals (whereas platinum <note>original: *Platina*</note>, on the other hand, is the most refractory <note>original: *strengflüssigsten*; meaning it requires extreme heat to melt</note>) because of its attraction to heat-matter <term>caloric</term> <note>original: *Wärmestoffe*; in the 18th century, heat was often theorized as a physical fluid or substance called 'caloric' that resided between the particles of matter</note>; Paracelsus \*\*\*\*) already [wrote] of this cause of fluidity:

...they often thought gold was born <note>original: *saepius putabant aurum nasci*</note>. One should also read Boerhaave's two excellent treatises concerning Mercury <note>original: *de Mercurio*</note>, in his minor works <note>original: *Opusculis*</note>; The Hague <note>original: *Hagae Com.*</note> 1738. Large quarto. Pages 129–139.

<meta>This page continues a discussion of Paracelsus’s theories on the physical nature of mercury, specifically addressing why it remains liquid at room temperature. It transitions from the previous page’s mention of mercury's freezing point to a direct quote from Paracelsus and later 18th-century reports of mercury "fixation."</meta>

[the flui]dity of mercury not entirely incorrectly, although he also mixes in incorrect ideas.

> “Conquered are those,” he says, “who hold concerning **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurio*</note> that it is of a wet and cold nature, or should be so, which it is not; rather, it is full of great heat and moisture, which heat and moisture are natural to it and cause it to always and constantly be in flux <term>liquid state</term>. For if it were of a cold and wet nature, it would have to be like frozen water, and always remain rigid and hard, and would first have to be brought to flux by the heat of fire, like other metals; but it does not require this, because it already has its fluidity from the heat that constantly keeps it in flux, by which it must always live and not die, or stiffen, or freeze, nor can it be fixed <term>solidified through alchemical means</term>.”

If mercury, according to the system of the phlogistonists <note>The phlogiston theory was a superseded chemical theory that posited a fire-like element called 'phlogiston' was contained within combustible bodies and released during combustion</note>, consists of mercury-calx <note>original: *Quecksilberkalke*; the powdery residue or oxide left after mercury is heated</note> and combustible matter (**phlogiston**), which is composed of heat-matter <term>caloric</term> and light-matter, then **Paracelsus** truly thought deeply on this matter.

In **Crell’s** *Chemical Annals* <note>original: *Crells chemischen Annalen*; a major 18th-century scientific journal</note> 1785, II. p. 483, and in **Wiegleb’s** *History of Chemistry* <note>original: *Wieglebs Geschichte der Chemie*</note>, II. p. 366, one reads that a **Madame d’Orbeslin** in **Paris** had invented a means to fix <term>fixation</term> <note>original: *figiren*; the process of turning a volatile substance into a solid that can withstand fire</note> mercury, so that even the strongest fire could no longer restore its volatility, and that it could be melted like other metals,

<vocab>Mercury, Mercury, Phlogiston, Paracelsus, Crell’s Chemical Annals, Wiegleb’s History of Chemistry, Madame d’Orbeslin, to fix/solidify, Alchemy</vocab>

<summary>This page evaluates Paracelsus's belief that mercury is inherently "hot" and "moist," which explains its liquid state, and connects this to 18th-century phlogiston theory and reports of successful mercury fixation.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, mercury, phlogiston, mercury-calx, fixation of mercury, Madame d'Orbeslin, history of chemistry, heat-matter</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fixation and chemical manipulation of mercury, moving from the alchemical views of Paracelsus to the 18th-century chemical experiments of Robert Boyle and Antoine Lavoisier.</meta>

...without adding any other metallic substan[ce]. <note>The right margin of this page is cropped, resulting in the loss of letters at the end of several lines; "substan" is likely "substance" (Substanz).</note>
But nothing more has been heard of it since.

The **Boylean method** of **oxidation**, or **calcination** <term>the process of heating a metal in air to convert it into a powdery oxide, then called a "calx"</term> of mercury—for which this metal is best suited—was taught by the great **Lavoisie[r]** <note>Antoine-Laurent de Lavoisier (1743–1794), often called the father of modern chemistry, who helped disprove the phlogiston theory</note> to be applied in a less dangerous manner *), whose earlier experiments are included in Mr. **Hermbstädt’s** <note>Sigismund Friedrich Hermbstädt (1760–1833), a German chemist who translated Lavoisier's work and helped spread modern chemical theory in Germany</note> translation on page 177 and following.

---

->*) *System of Antiphlogistic Chemistry* <note>original: *System der antiphlogistischen Chemie*; Lavoisier's landmark work which rejected the "phlogiston" theory of combustion</note>, 3rd Part, p. 170 of the German translation, 1792.<-

<summary>This page records the historical transition in chemical methodology regarding mercury oxidation, moving from Robert Boyle's earlier techniques to Antoine Lavoisier's refined, less hazardous "antiphlogistic" approach.</summary>
